# Daily Summary: 2025-06-15

### One For All: LLM-based Heterogeneous Mission Planning in Precision Agriculture
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10106v1)
- **Authors**: Marcos Abel Zuzuárregui, Mustafa Melih Toslak, Stefano Carpin
- **Abstract**: Artificial intelligence is transforming precision agriculture, offering farmers new tools to streamline their daily operations. While these technological advances promise increased efficiency, they often introduce additional complexity and steep learning curves that are particularly challenging for non-technical users who must balance tech adoption with existing workloads. In this paper, we present a natural language (NL) robotic mission planner that enables non-specialists to control heterogeneous robots through a common interface. By leveraging large language models (LLMs) and predefined primitives, our architecture seamlessly translates human language into intermediate descriptions that can be executed by different robotic platforms. With this system, users can formulate complex agricultural missions without writing any code. In the work presented in this paper, we extend our previous system tailored for wheeled robot mission planning through a new class of experiments involving robotic manipulation and computer vision tasks. Our results demonstrate that the architecture is both general enough to support a diverse set of robots and powerful enough to execute complex mission requests. This work represents a significant step toward making robotic automation in precision agriculture more accessible to non-technical users.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents an architecture for robotic mission planning (MP) in precision agriculture that uses large language models (LLMs) to translate natural language queries into robot actions. The key goal is to enable non-technical users to control heterogeneous robots (wheeled robots and manipulators) through a common, user-friendly interface. The system uses predefined primitives (ROS2 actions) and constrains LLM outputs using XML schema definitions (XSDs) to ensure robust task definitions and avoid inaccuracies. The architecture aims for "one-shot" planning, suitable for environments with limited network connectivity. The paper extends a previous system (focused on wheeled robots) by incorporating robotic manipulation and computer vision tasks, demonstrating its potential for diverse agricultural missions. The authors emphasize modularity and flexibility, allowing the system to adapt to new robots and capabilities by only updating a limited set of atomic action definitions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Practical Problem and Target User:** The paper addresses a real-world problem in precision agriculture: the difficulty for non-technical users to interact with complex robotic systems.  This focus on accessibility is commendable.
*   **Heterogeneous Robot Control:**  The architecture's ability to control both wheeled robots and manipulators from a single interface is a significant step forward.  Demonstrating this capability with field experiments adds credibility.
*   **XSD Constraint and Modularity:**  The use of XSDs to constrain LLM outputs is a smart approach to mitigate LLM's tendency to produce inaccurate or unstructured results. The modular design and the concept of atomic tasks enhance reusability and adaptability. This is a key strength. The idea of a predefined action pool, which the LLM combines, limits the open-endedness of an LLM and provides some assurances about the end result.
*   **Standardized Workflow (IEEE 1872.1-2024):** Adhering to an established standard for robot task representation adds to the framework's potential for wider adoption.
*   **"One-Shot" Planning:** The focus on "one-shot" planning in the context of limited network connectivity is a relevant and crucial constraint for many agricultural applications.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty of LLM Use:** The core idea of using LLMs for mission planning isn't entirely new.  Many papers have explored this, including some cited by the authors. The incremental contribution lies in the specific application (precision agriculture with heterogeneous robots) and the emphasis on modularity and constraint.
*   **Reliance on OpenAI's GPT:** The system's dependence on OpenAI's ChatGPT limits its accessibility and raises concerns about long-term reliability and potential vendor lock-in. Exploring alternative LLMs (as the authors mention they plan to do) is essential.
*   **Spatial Reasoning Limitations:** The reported difficulties with spatial reasoning and kinematics highlight a significant challenge for LLM-based planning. Overcoming this requires further research and likely the integration of specialized modules (as suggested by the authors' mention of GNNs). Kinematics, in particular, seems problematic with this system. This has not been solved by the modular system design.
*   **Vagueness in Queries:** Vagueness is a problem in every LLM implementation. The authors do not provide a strong solution to this beyond explicit querying.
*   **Limited Evaluation Depth:** While the experiments demonstrate basic functionality, a more in-depth evaluation of the system's robustness, scalability, and performance under various environmental conditions would strengthen the paper. The success metric is "semantic success", which is too subjective. More concrete metrics, such as task completion time and error rate would be preferred.
*   **Atomic tasks:** The choice of what is "atomic" will invariably cause issues. A bad choice could affect the LLM's capacity to generate adequate plans.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance lies in its pragmatic approach to applying LLMs to a challenging real-world problem. The focus on non-technical users, heterogeneous robot control, and modular design addresses key barriers to the adoption of robotic automation in agriculture. The emphasis on standardization and robust task definition makes the system more likely to be deployed and scaled. However, the limitations with spatial reasoning and dependence on external LLM providers need to be addressed to realize its full potential.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper has several strengths, the limited novelty of the core LLM-based planning approach and the acknowledged limitations with spatial reasoning prevent it from achieving a higher score. The modular design and focus on practical constraints are valuable contributions, but the paper needs more robust evaluation and further development to overcome its weaknesses. The issues with kinematics, in particular, are not solved by the modular architecture.

### AI5GTest: AI-Driven Specification-Aware Automated Testing and Validation of 5G O-RAN Components
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10111v1)
- **Authors**: Abiodun Ganiyu, Pranshav Gajjar, Vijay K Shah
- **Abstract**: The advent of Open Radio Access Networks (O-RAN) has transformed the telecommunications industry by promoting interoperability, vendor diversity, and rapid innovation. However, its disaggregated architecture introduces complex testing challenges, particularly in validating multi-vendor components against O-RAN ALLIANCE and 3GPP specifications. Existing frameworks, such as those provided by Open Testing and Integration Centres (OTICs), rely heavily on manual processes, are fragmented and prone to human error, leading to inconsistency and scalability issues. To address these limitations, we present AI5GTest -- an AI-powered, specification-aware testing framework designed to automate the validation of O-RAN components. AI5GTest leverages a cooperative Large Language Models (LLM) framework consisting of Gen-LLM, Val-LLM, and Debug-LLM. Gen-LLM automatically generates expected procedural flows for test cases based on 3GPP and O-RAN specifications, while Val-LLM cross-references signaling messages against these flows to validate compliance and detect deviations. If anomalies arise, Debug-LLM performs root cause analysis, providing insight to the failure cause. To enhance transparency and trustworthiness, AI5GTest incorporates a human-in-the-loop mechanism, where the Gen-LLM presents top-k relevant official specifications to the tester for approval before proceeding with validation. Evaluated using a range of test cases obtained from O-RAN TIFG and WG5-IOT test specifications, AI5GTest demonstrates a significant reduction in overall test execution time compared to traditional manual methods, while maintaining high validation accuracy.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "AI5GTest: AI-Driven Specification-Aware Automated Testing and Validation of 5G O-RAN Components," with a justified novelty and significance score.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AI5GTest, an AI-powered framework designed to automate the testing and validation of 5G Open Radio Access Network (O-RAN) components. Addressing the challenges of manual, error-prone, and fragmented testing approaches in O-RAN deployments, AI5GTest leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate expected procedural flows from 3GPP and O-RAN specifications (Gen-LLM), validate signaling messages against these flows (Val-LLM), and identify root causes of failures (Debug-LLM). A human-in-the-loop mechanism is included to ensure transparency and tester approval of generated specifications. The framework is evaluated on O-RAN test cases, showing a substantial reduction in testing time compared to manual methods while maintaining high validation accuracy.  The framework includes a centralized repository of test cases, a packet analyzer (PCAP Analyzer), and a test orchestrator, providing a scalable and automated testing environment.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

The paper addresses a real and growing problem in the telecommunications industry: the complexity of testing and validating O-RAN components in multi-vendor environments. While various testing frameworks exist, their reliance on manual processes creates bottlenecks and inconsistencies. The proposed AI5GTest attempts to fill the gap by automating key aspects of the testing process using LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Automation of Key Processes:**  The paper demonstrates the potential of LLMs to automate the generation of test procedures, validation of signaling messages, and root cause analysis. This can significantly reduce human effort and improve the consistency of testing results.
    *   **Specification-Awareness:** The framework’s reliance on 3GPP and O-RAN specifications ensures compliance and reduces the risk of vendor-specific interpretations.
    *   **Human-in-the-Loop:** This adds an element of trust and transparency, mitigating concerns about the black-box nature of AI-driven decision-making.
    *   **Modular Architecture:**  The separation of concerns among Gen-LLM, Val-LLM, and Debug-LLM makes the framework more maintainable and extensible.
    *   **Evaluation:** The testbed and use of existing O-RAN test specifications provide a baseline for assessment. Performance gains of significantly shorter test times is valuable.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **LLM Dependency:** The framework's effectiveness heavily depends on the accuracy and comprehensiveness of the LLMs used, the quality of the training data/specifications it can access, and the sophistication of the prompts used. LLM inaccuracies or ambiguities in specifications could lead to incorrect procedural flows and validation results.  The paper highlights this dependence by only using "open-source" tools, while closed-source LLMs have already been proven effective in adjacent spaces.
    *   **Generalizability:** While the paper claims platform independence based on reliance on the standards, the evaluation uses only one O-RAN implementation (srsRAN). Performance across diverse O-RAN vendor components (with different implementations of the specifications) remains to be seen.
    *   **Limited Evaluation Scope:** The evaluation covers a reasonable set of test cases, but further testing with larger, more complex scenarios and a wider range of vendor equipment is needed to fully assess the framework's scalability and robustness. The evaluation of "failure states" is essentially simulated failures; real failures often arise from subtle, unforeseen interactions between components.
    *   **Overclaims of 100% accuracy:** Based on the information presented, achieving complete validation correctness is unlikely due to issues in specifications. There should have been more discussion of the quality of the specifications, and corner cases where the AI might misinterpret the protocol.

*   **Novelty:**

    *   The combination of procedural flow generation, validation, and root cause analysis, driven by LLMs within the O-RAN domain, represents a novel approach. Integrating a human-in-the-loop is a sensible and pragmatic design choice.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The potential impact on the O-RAN ecosystem is high. Faster, more consistent, and reliable testing could accelerate the adoption of O-RAN by reducing integration risks and costs.
    *   The work could inspire further research on applying AI/ML techniques to automate various aspects of network management and optimization in disaggregated network environments.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution by demonstrating the feasibility of using LLMs to automate O-RAN testing. The proposed AI5GTest framework has the potential to address key challenges in O-RAN deployment. The main weakness lies in the limited scope of validation and the heavy dependence on open-source LLM quality and data availability.

Score: 7

**Rigorous Rationale:** The score of 7 reflects the paper's solid technical contribution and potential impact while acknowledging its current limitations. The innovation is significant, and the problem addressed is important. However, the reliance on specific LLM characteristics and the limited scale of the evaluation warrant a degree of caution. The paper's future significance will depend on demonstrating the framework's generalizability, scalability, and robustness in real-world O-RAN deployments with diverse vendor components. The score emphasizes that the paper is a significant *step* towards AI-driven O-RAN testing, not a *definitive solution*.
- **Classification**: cs.NI
- **Score**: 7/10

### ChartReasoner: Code-Driven Modality Bridging for Long-Chain Reasoning in Chart Question Answering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10116v1)
- **Authors**: Caijun Jia, Nan Xu, Jingxuan Wei, Qingli Wang, Lei Wang, Bihui Yu, Junnan Zhu
- **Abstract**: Recently, large language models have shown remarkable reasoning capabilities through long-chain reasoning before responding. However, how to extend this capability to visual reasoning tasks remains an open challenge. Existing multimodal reasoning approaches transfer such visual reasoning task into textual reasoning task via several image-to-text conversions, which often lose critical structural and semantic information embedded in visualizations, especially for tasks like chart question answering that require a large amount of visual details. To bridge this gap, we propose ChartReasoner, a code-driven novel two-stage framework designed to enable precise, interpretable reasoning over charts. We first train a high-fidelity model to convert diverse chart images into structured ECharts codes, preserving both layout and data semantics as lossless as possible. Then, we design a general chart reasoning data synthesis pipeline, which leverages this pretrained transport model to automatically and scalably generate chart reasoning trajectories and utilizes a code validator to filter out low-quality samples. Finally, we train the final multimodal model using a combination of supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning on our synthesized chart reasoning dataset and experimental results on four public benchmarks clearly demonstrate the effectiveness of our proposed ChartReasoner. It can preserve the original details of the charts as much as possible and perform comparably with state-of-the-art open-source models while using fewer parameters, approaching the performance of proprietary systems like GPT-4o in out-of-domain settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "ChartReasoner: Code-Driven Modality Bridging for Long-Chain Reasoning in Chart Question Answering" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ChartReasoner, a two-stage framework for chart question answering (ChartQA) that aims to address the limitations of existing multimodal reasoning approaches. These approaches often rely on lossy image-to-text conversions, leading to the loss of crucial structural and semantic information. ChartReasoner's key idea is to bridge the visual-textual modality gap by first converting chart images into structured ECharts code using a trained model called Chart2Code. This code-based representation preserves layout and data semantics more faithfully. The second stage involves creating a large-scale chart reasoning dataset (ChartThink) by applying Chart2Code to existing ChartQA datasets and prompting a language model to generate reasoning traces.  Finally, the ChartReasoner model is trained using supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning (RL) to improve reasoning accuracy, consistency, and interpretability. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of their approach on four public benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the code-driven approach to chart understanding. While code-centric approaches for charts exist (e.g., ChartCoder), ChartReasoner's ability to transform complex chart images to structured ECharts code for downstream reasoning tasks appears significant. The data synthesis pipeline using prompt-based generation and a code validator contributes to the scalability and quality of the generated data.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical challenge in visual reasoning: preserving structural and semantic information during modality conversion. By representing charts as executable code, ChartReasoner enables more precise and interpretable reasoning. The results on public benchmarks suggest the framework is competitive with existing open-source models and approaches proprietary systems like GPT-4 in out-of-domain scenarios.  This advancement could greatly help in real-world applications where reasoning and understanding of visually presented information are essential.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Code-driven approach:** The primary strength lies in using ECharts code as an intermediate representation, minimizing information loss.
    *   **Scalable data synthesis:**  The ability to automatically generate large-scale chart reasoning datasets is a significant advantage.
    *   **Strong performance:** The experimental results on multiple benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed framework.
    *   **Interpretability:** The code-based representation should improve the interpretability of the reasoning process.
    *   **Out-of-domain capabilities:** ChartReasoner demonstrates effective generalization to out-of-domain charts.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on DeepSeek-R1**: The study relies heavily on DeepSeek-R1 to generate the initial data.
    *   **Limited Model Evaluation**: All experiments are done using a 7B parameter model and thus more extensive investigation of different model configurations would've strengthened the paper.
    *   **Dependency on Code Translation Accuracy:**  The entire framework relies on the accuracy of the Chart2Code model. Errors in code translation will propagate to the reasoning stage.
    *   **Synthetic Data Bias:** The training data generated using prompts might introduce biases that affect the model's generalization to more complex or real-world charts. The paper acknowledges a sensitivity to chart type.
    *   **Evaluation mostly on synthetic or semi-structured charts:**  The paper itself claims the generalization of the method to more complex, real-world visualizations remains open.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   The code-driven approach could inspire new research directions in visual reasoning and multimodal learning.
    *   The ChartThink dataset could serve as a valuable resource for training and evaluating chart reasoning models.
    *   The framework can help real-world applications that require visual reasoning capabilities.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a well-motivated and executed approach to chart question answering. The code-driven modality bridging offers a promising solution to the limitations of existing approaches. The experimental results are compelling, and the potential impact on the field is significant. However, potential biases from the synthetic data, reliance on deepseek, and scope for scaling limits its impact.

### Detecção da Psoríase Utilizando Visão Computacional: Uma Abordagem Comparativa Entre CNNs e Vision Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10119v1)
- **Authors**: Natanael Lucena, Fábio S. da Silva, Ricardo Rios
- **Abstract**: This paper presents a comparison of the performance of Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) and Vision Transformers (ViTs) in the task of multi-classifying images containing lesions of psoriasis and diseases similar to it. Models pre-trained on ImageNet were adapted to a specific data set. Both achieved high predictive metrics, but the ViTs stood out for their superior performance with smaller models. Dual Attention Vision Transformer-Base (DaViT-B) obtained the best results, with an f1-score of 96.4%, and is recommended as the most efficient architecture for automated psoriasis detection. This article reinforces the potential of ViTs for medical image classification tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper compares the performance of Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) and Vision Transformers (ViTs) for multi-classifying images of psoriasis lesions and similar skin diseases. Pre-trained models on ImageNet were adapted and fine-tuned on a specific dataset. Both CNNs and ViTs achieved high prediction accuracy, but the ViTs demonstrated superior performance with smaller models. Specifically, the Dual Attention Vision Transformer-Base (DaViT-B) model achieved the best results with an F1-score of 96.4% and is recommended as an efficient architecture for automated psoriasis detection. The authors aim to support dermatological diagnosis using computer vision techniques, addressing the challenges of visual similarity between psoriasis and other skin conditions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper addresses a relevant clinical problem: differentiating psoriasis from visually similar skin diseases.
    *   **Comparative Analysis:** The study provides a direct comparison between CNNs and ViTs, which is valuable for understanding the applicability of different architectures to this specific task.
    *   **Strong Results:** The DaViT-B model achieves a high F1-score, indicating promising performance for automated detection. The F1-score is a particularly appropriate metric, as it helps to account for a potentially imbalanced dataset with minority and majority classes.
    *   **Recommendation:** The authors provide a concrete recommendation (DaViT-B) based on the empirical results.
    *   **ViT Exploration:** Investigating Vision Transformers in this context is well-aligned with current trends in computer vision research, and exploring ViTs helps the study remain novel.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dataset Concerns:** The method of data collection raises questions. Specifically, the usage of public repositories and Google Images. While using publicly available images is a common practice, the heterogeneity of such datasets can lead to biases that need to be carefully addressed. The paper does not specify the usage of the repositories. The use of google images as the source of the data also poses questions regarding the data diversity, considering that they are not annotated. Also, if the images are publicly available, this would allow for other members of the scientific community to repeat the study.
    *   **Limited Novelty in Methodology:** The methodology mainly involves fine-tuning pre-trained models. There is no specific methodological innovation or enhancement proposed.
    *   **Comparatively Small Model Set:** The number of models compared, while reasonable, could be expanded. A more thorough investigation of different CNN and ViT variants would strengthen the claims. For example, the authors should specify why they only tested ViTs instead of Swin-transformers, as the latter has previously demonstrated strong performance.
    *   **Limited Discussion on Generalizability:** While the F1-score is good, there is limited discussion on the generalizability of the model to different demographic groups, skin tones, image qualities, or clinical settings. This is crucial in medical applications. The dataset is not described in sufficient detail to assess the real-world applicability of the model.
    *   **No Ablation Studies:** The paper lacks ablation studies to understand the individual contributions of different components of the models (e.g., the "Dual Attention" in DaViT-B). This limits the insights gained.
    *   **Limited Practical Validation:** The paper lacks validation in real-world clinical settings with expert dermatologists. The system's utility and acceptance by clinicians remain unproven.
    *   **No discussion in regards to explainability**: In the field of medical diagnostic tools, it is of high importance to understand why a particular output was generated, which is usually done through Explainable AI.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper contributes to the application of computer vision in medical image analysis, specifically for dermatology.
    *   It provides empirical evidence for the effectiveness of ViTs in this domain, which could stimulate further research.
    *   The recommended DaViT-B model could serve as a baseline for future studies on automated psoriasis detection.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a worthwhile comparison of CNNs and ViTs for psoriasis detection with promising results. The DaViT-B model achieves a high F1-score. However, the limitations in dataset management, methodological novelty, absence of ablation studies, a limited practical validation, as well as the omission of modern topics such as explainability, detract significantly from the paper's overall impact. Thus, a more significant step change in the data, method, and evaluation would be necessary for a higher evaluation.

**Score: 5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 5/10

### D-LiFT: Improving LLM-based Decompiler Backend via Code Quality-driven Fine-tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10125v1)
- **Authors**: Muqi Zou, Hongyu Cai, Hongwei Wu, Zion Leonahenahe Basque, Arslan Khan, Berkay Celik, Dave, Tian, Antonio Bianchi, Ruoyu, Wang, Dongyan Xu
- **Abstract**: Decompilers, which reconstruct human-readable source code from binary executables, are vital to many security tasks. Yet, despite recent advances, their output often suffers from syntactic and semantic errors and remains difficult to read. Recently, with the advent of large language models (LLMs), researchers began to explore the potential of LLMs to refine decompiler output. Nevertheless, our study of these approaches reveals significant limitations, such as introducing new errors and relying on unreliable accuracy validation. In this paper, we present D-LiFT, an automated decompiler backend that harnesses and further trains LLMs to improve the quality of decompiled code via reinforcement learning (RL). Unlike prior work that overlooks preserving accuracy, D-LiFT adheres to a key principle for enhancing the quality of decompiled code: \textit{preserving accuracy while improving readability}. Central to D-LiFT, we propose D-SCORE, an integrated quality assessment system to score the decompiled code from multiple aspects. In line with our principle, D-SCORE assigns low scores to any inaccurate output and only awards higher scores for readability to code that passes the accuracy check. Specifically, D-SCORE first verifies the syntactic and semantic correctness via the compiler and symbolic execution; only if a candidate is deemed accurate, it then evaluates readability using established metrics to compare the LLM output with the original decompiled code. The score will then be fed back to the LLM for fine-tuning. Our implementation, based on Ghidra and a range of LLMs, demonstrates significant improvements for the accurate decompiled code from the coreutils and util-linux projects. Compared to baseline LLMs without D-SCORE-driven fine-tuning, D-LiFT produces 55.3% more improved decompiled functions, as measured by D-SCORE.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces D-LIFT, an automated decompiler backend that leverages reinforcement learning (RL) to improve the quality of decompiled code generated by Large Language Models (LLMs).  D-LIFT's core principle is preserving accuracy while enhancing readability. It employs D-SCORE, a novel code quality assessment system, that integrates syntactic, semantic correctness checks, and readability metrics to provide feedback for LLM fine-tuning.  D-SCORE penalizes inaccurate code and rewards readability only when accuracy is guaranteed.  The authors implement D-LIFT using Ghidra and various LLMs, demonstrating improved decompilation results on code from the coreutils and util-linux projects. They show a significant increase in the number of improved functions compared to baseline LLMs and improved overall selection of best outputs across baseline, fine-tuned, and original decompiled code.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers moderate novelty. The core idea of using RL to fine-tune LLMs for decompilation is not entirely new, as prior works like LLM4Decompile and DecGPT have explored LLM-assisted decompilation. However, D-LIFT's primary innovation lies in the design of D-SCORE.  The integration of syntax checks (via a recompiler), semantic checks (via symbolic execution, though borrowing heavily from D-helix), and readability metrics into a cohesive reward function is a valuable contribution.  The explicit focus on *preserving accuracy* as a precondition for rewarding readability is also a key distinction from previous approaches that often sacrificed correctness for stylistic improvements.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem: LLMs can improve decompiled code readability, but often introduce inaccuracies. D-LIFT's emphasis on accuracy and its method for achieving it directly addresses this. The experimental results, demonstrating substantial improvements in the number of improved functions and the selection of the "best" decompilation, suggest a practical impact. The observation regarding the 'improve-ability gap' between originally accurate vs inaccurate outputs from decompiler is valuable. This emphasizes the importance of the decompiler front-end and the inherent limitations of current LLMs to "fix" significantly flawed decompilations.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Emphasis on Accuracy:**  The paper clearly articulates and adheres to the critical principle of preserving accuracy, which is often overlooked in other LLM-based code generation efforts.
    *   **D-SCORE Design:** D-SCORE provides a well-structured and reasoned approach to scoring decompiled code, integrating diverse elements into a unified reward function. The use of re-compilation and symbolic execution is sound, leveraging existing tools effectively.
    *   **Experimental Evaluation:** The paper employs a reasonably rigorous experimental setup, using established benchmark projects (coreutils and util-linux) and comparing against baseline LLMs.
    *   **Error Analysis:**  The manual error analysis to understand the causes of both improvements and regressions is valuable and provides insight into the limitations of current LLMs in this domain.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on D-helix:** The reliance on D-helix's framework for syntax and semantic checking, while pragmatic, inherits some of its limitations.  Specifically, D-helix struggles with floating point, large binaries, etc. and can miss errors, which directly impacts D-SCORE's accuracy. This is a key limitation, especially for more complex binaries.
    *   **Limited Semantic Coverage:**  The semantic equivalence check, while using symbolic execution, is not exhaustive. The paper acknowledges limitations with external function call modeling, which uses simplifying assumptions.  There is a discussion on why function argument models is ignored, which affects the quality. The metric is simplified and not comprehensive, so is probably missing many cases.
    *   **Readability Metric Reliance on Existing Metrics:**  The readability assessment is a weighted average of existing metrics (B&W and R2I). While this is a practical approach, it doesn't represent a fundamental advance in readability assessment itself. The R2I metrics were designed for measuring differences across decompilers, not for the fine-grained assessment of small improvements from LLM-based refinement.
    *   **Scalability Concerns:** The RL training process, particularly with symbolic execution as part of the reward function, is computationally expensive.  This limits the size of the training dataset and the scale of problems D-LIFT can tackle.
    *   **Limited generalizability analysis:** The authors only showed it works for two projects (coreutils and util-linux), both from the GNU core. It's unclear how it would generalize to closed source projects (or other OSS, such as BSD's).

*   **Potential Influence:** D-LIFT highlights the importance of accuracy-preserving LLM-based code generation.  The D-SCORE methodology could be adapted and extended to other code generation tasks. The insights regarding limitations (the "improve-ability gap") could guide future research in decompiler design and more robust LLM training.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper makes a solid contribution by addressing a crucial limitation of LLM-assisted decompilation – the introduction of inaccuracies. The integration of accuracy checks into the reward function (D-SCORE) is a valuable contribution. However, the reliance on D-helix and the non-exhaustive semantic coverage, coupled with the somewhat incremental nature of the readability assessment, tempers the novelty and significance. While D-LIFT demonstrates improvements over baseline LLMs, its ultimate effectiveness is constrained by the quality of both the decompiler front-end and the symbolic execution framework. This limits its potential for revolutionary impact. The analysis in this paper would be extremely valuable, however, when considering other LLM-based decompilation approaches, because it provides a framework for improving LLM accuracy and readability.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### ViCrit: A Verifiable Reinforcement Learning Proxy Task for Visual Perception in VLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10128v1)
- **Authors**: Xiyao Wang, Zhengyuan Yang, Chao Feng, Yongyuan Liang, Yuhang Zhou, Xiaoyu Liu, Ziyi Zang, Ming Li, Chung-Ching Lin, Kevin Lin, Linjie Li, Furong Huang, Lijuan Wang
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning (RL) has shown great effectiveness for fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) using tasks that are challenging yet easily verifiable, such as math reasoning or code generation. However, extending this success to visual perception in vision-language models (VLMs) has been impeded by the scarcity of vision-centric tasks that are simultaneously challenging and unambiguously verifiable. To this end, we introduce ViCrit (Visual Caption Hallucination Critic), an RL proxy task that trains VLMs to localize a subtle, synthetic visual hallucination injected into paragraphs of human-written image captions. Starting from a 200-word captions, we inject a single, subtle visual description error-altering a few words on objects, attributes, counts, or spatial relations-and task the model to pinpoint the corrupted span given the image and the modified caption. This formulation preserves the full perceptual difficulty while providing a binary, exact-match reward that is easy to compute and unambiguous. Models trained with the ViCrit Task exhibit substantial gains across a variety of VL benchmarks. Crucially, the improvements transfer beyond natural-image training data to abstract image reasoning and visual math, showing promises of learning to perceive rather than barely memorizing seen objects. To facilitate evaluation, we further introduce ViCrit-Bench, a category-balanced diagnostic benchmark that systematically probes perception errors across diverse image domains and error types. Together, our results demonstrate that fine-grained hallucination criticism is an effective and generalizable objective for enhancing visual perception in VLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ViCrit: A Verifiable Reinforcement Learning Proxy Task for Visual Perception in VLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ViCrit, a novel reinforcement learning (RL) proxy task designed to improve visual perception in Vision-Language Models (VLMs). The core idea is to train VLMs to identify subtle, synthetically injected visual hallucinations within otherwise accurate, human-written image captions. Starting with detailed captions (200+ words), the method injects a single, subtle visual description error related to objects, attributes, counts, or spatial relationships.  The model's task is to pinpoint the corrupted span given the image and the modified caption, rewarded with a binary, exact-match score.  The authors demonstrate that training with ViCrit leads to significant gains across various VL benchmarks and improves performance even on abstract image reasoning and visual math tasks, suggesting the model learns to *perceive* rather than just memorize.  They also introduce ViCrit-Bench, a diagnostic benchmark for evaluating VLMs on hallucination detection.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the creation of a *verifiable* RL proxy task for visual perception.  While RL for language models and supervised training on large captioning datasets are established, using RL specifically to *criticize* or identify errors in captions to improve visual grounding is a more recent approach. The design of the ViCrit task, specifically targeting subtle hallucinations within detailed captions, provides a valuable contribution. The creation of ViCrit-Bench is also a valuable addition, offering a more granular and systematic way to evaluate VLMs' visual perception capabilities.
*   **Significance:** The work addresses a crucial challenge in VLMs: the lack of readily verifiable tasks that demand fine-grained visual perception.  Many VQA and captioning tasks can be solved with relatively shallow scene understanding. The results suggest that ViCrit promotes the development of internal perception strategies that go beyond mere object recognition and enable cross-checking textual claims against visual evidence. The improvements demonstrated across a diverse range of VL benchmarks and in abstract image reasoning scenarios support the significance of the approach.  The development of ViCrit-Bench makes this paper more valuable. The experimental design could be enhanced by explicitly comparing against captioning finetuning in RL setting or self-supervised learning settings.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clearly Defined Task:** The ViCrit task is well-defined, enabling easy computation of rewards.
    *   **Effective RL Training:** The paper demonstrated that RL using the ViCrit task can lead to improved performance across a range of tasks.
    *   **Generalizability:** The improvement extended to abstract image reasoning and visual math, which were not used during training.
    *   **ViCrit-Bench:** The introduction of the ViCrit-Bench facilitates systematic evaluation of VLM perception errors.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Synthetic Hallucinations:** The hallucinations are *synthetically* injected. While this enables controlled experiments and verifiable rewards, it is less clear whether the skills transfer fully to real-world hallucination scenarios. More complex and natural hallucinations may need to be addressed in future works.
    *   **Potential Data Imbalance:** As the authors point out, there may be data imbalances, such as the limited number of charts and diagrams in the training set.
    *   **Reliance on GPT-4 for Hallucination Generation:** The approach depends on GPT-4 to generate plausible visual hallucinations. Though using a large language model automates much of this process, this dependency introduces a constraint on what types of visual errors can be injected into the captions.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more robust and reliable VLMs. The ViCrit task provides a practical and scalable method for improving visual grounding, and the ViCrit-Bench enables a more granular understanding of VLM perception capabilities. Other RL proxy tasks will likely be designed based on the principles outlined in this paper.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed RL proxy task that addresses a critical issue in the VLM field: improving fine-grained visual perception through a verifiable learning paradigm. The empirical results, particularly the improvement in abstract reasoning and visual math tasks, and the introduction of ViCrit-Bench demonstrate the value of the approach. While relying on synthetic hallucinations and GPT-4 for hallucination generation are limitations, the significant contributions and potential influence warrant a score of 7.5. It's a solid contribution that is not groundbreaking but very practical and useful for the community.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Diffusion prior as a direct regularization term for FWI
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10141v1)
- **Authors**: Yuke Xie, Hervé Chauris, Nicolas Desassis
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have recently shown promise as powerful generative priors for inverse problems. However, conventional applications require solving the full reverse diffusion process and operating on noisy intermediate states, which poses challenges for physics-constrained computational seismic imaging. In particular, such instability is pronounced in non-linear solvers like those used in Full Waveform Inversion (FWI), where wave propagation through noisy velocity fields can lead to numerical artifacts and poor inversion quality. In this work, we propose a simple yet effective framework that directly integrates a pretrained Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Model (DDPM) as a score-based generative diffusion prior into FWI through a score rematching strategy. Unlike traditional diffusion approaches, our method avoids the reverse diffusion sampling and needs fewer iterations. We operate the image inversion entirely in the clean image space, eliminating the need to operate through noisy velocity models. The generative diffusion prior can be introduced as a simple regularization term in the standard FWI update rule, requiring minimal modification to existing FWI pipelines. This promotes stable wave propagation and can improve convergence behavior and inversion quality. Numerical experiments suggest that the proposed method offers enhanced fidelity and robustness compared to conventional and GAN-based FWI approaches, while remaining practical and computationally efficient for seismic imaging and other inverse problem tasks.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach for incorporating Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models (DDPMs) into Full Waveform Inversion (FWI). Instead of using DDPMs in the conventional way, which involves operating in noisy latent spaces and solving the reverse diffusion process, the authors propose using a pre-trained DDPM denoiser directly as a regularization term in the FWI optimization. This "score-rematching" strategy avoids working with noisy intermediate velocity models, leading to more stable and computationally efficient inversions.  The generative diffusion prior is introduced as a simple regularization term in the standard FWI update rule. Numerical experiments on synthetic seismic data demonstrate that this method enhances fidelity, robustness, and convergence compared to conventional and GAN-based FWI approaches. The paper also explores the generalization capability of the method and addresses the issue of uncertainty quantification and interpretability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its innovative integration of a DDPM denoiser as a direct regularization term within the FWI framework, specifically through a score-rematching approach. While using generative models as priors for inverse problems is not entirely new, the authors present a unique and practical way to leverage DDPMs, avoiding the instability and computational burden associated with reverse diffusion sampling. Compared to other generative models like GANs that have been used in the past, this approach demonstrates the potential to better leverage the power of DDPMs. This is a significant departure from traditional diffusion-based inverse problem methods. Furthermore, the strategy to avoid operations with noisy latent states is a key advantage.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work is multifaceted:

    *   **Improved Stability and Convergence:** FWI is a notoriously challenging optimization problem. By operating directly in the smooth image space and leveraging the DDPM's denoising capabilities, the proposed method promotes more stable wave propagation, which is crucial for accurate and robust inversions. The experiments also demonstrate improved convergence speed.
    *   **Computational Efficiency:**  Avoiding reverse diffusion sampling significantly reduces the computational cost compared to conventional DDPM-based inverse problem solvers. This makes the approach more practical for large-scale seismic imaging applications.
    *   **Ease of Integration:** The method can be easily integrated into existing FWI pipelines as a simple regularization term, requiring minimal modifications. This increases its potential for adoption in practice.
    *   **Generalization:** The authors address the important issue of generalization and show the potential for retraining DDPMs on more diverse datasets to improve performance on unseen geological structures. While the improvements are demonstrated to be a significant potential contribution, the improvement on generalization is incremental.
    *   **Practicality:** The method has a straightforward implementation and does not introduce solver-specific issues, compared to other deep learning approaches like implicit layers.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear and well-structured paper with a thorough explanation of the proposed method.
    *   Convincing experimental results that demonstrate the advantages of the diffusion-prior FWI over conventional methods.
    *   Addresses important practical considerations such as stability, convergence, computational efficiency, and generalization.
    *   Discusses limitations related to training data dependence and the interpretability of the learned prior.
    *   The code is made available, improving reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Experiments are primarily on synthetic data. While sufficient for a proof-of-concept, more real-world datasets would strengthen the claims.
    *   The discussion on hyperparameter tuning (the weight of the regularization term) could be more detailed. Adaptive strategies are mentioned but not explored.
    *   Uncertainty quantification is mentioned as a future direction but not addressed in the present work. This is an important aspect for practical applications of FWI.
    *   While the work on generalization improves the performance in Marmousi2 dataset, it's still a relatively small shift and a more thorough benchmark against a wider range of geological datasets would strengthen this aspect.

*   **Potential Impact:**

    The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field of seismic imaging by providing a more stable, efficient, and robust approach to FWI using diffusion models. It could enable the reconstruction of higher-resolution subsurface models with improved accuracy, which is crucial for resource exploration, hazard assessment, and other geophysical applications. The ease of integration into existing workflows is a significant advantage.

*   **Justification for the Score:**

    The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to integrating DDPMs into FWI. The benefits in terms of stability, convergence, and computational efficiency are clearly demonstrated. While there are areas for improvement, such as more extensive real-world validation, uncertainty quantification and a more thorough benchmark regarding the generalization capability of the model, the paper represents a substantial contribution to the field. The potential impact on practical seismic imaging is significant. I'm awarding the score taking into account the relative significance of the strengths vs. the weaknesses.

    **Score: 8**

- **Classification**: physics.geo-ph
- **Score**: 8/10

### RoCA: Robust Cross-Domain End-to-End Autonomous Driving
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10145v1)
- **Authors**: Rajeev Yasarla, Shizhong Han, Hsin-Pai Cheng, Litian Liu, Shweta Mahajan, Apratim Bhattacharyya, Yunxiao Shi, Risheek Garrepalli, Hong Cai, Fatih Porikli
- **Abstract**: End-to-end (E2E) autonomous driving has recently emerged as a new paradigm, offering significant potential. However, few studies have looked into the practical challenge of deployment across domains (e.g., cities). Although several works have incorporated Large Language Models (LLMs) to leverage their open-world knowledge, LLMs do not guarantee cross-domain driving performance and may incur prohibitive retraining costs during domain adaptation. In this paper, we propose RoCA, a novel framework for robust cross-domain E2E autonomous driving. RoCA formulates the joint probabilistic distribution over the tokens that encode ego and surrounding vehicle information in the E2E pipeline. Instantiating with a Gaussian process (GP), RoCA learns a set of basis tokens with corresponding trajectories, which span diverse driving scenarios. Then, given any driving scene, it is able to probabilistically infer the future trajectory. By using RoCA together with a base E2E model in source-domain training, we improve the generalizability of the base model, without requiring extra inference computation. In addition, RoCA enables robust adaptation on new target domains, significantly outperforming direct finetuning. We extensively evaluate RoCA on various cross-domain scenarios and show that it achieves strong domain generalization and adaptation performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RoCA (Robust Cross-domain End-to-end Autonomous Driving), a novel framework designed to improve the robustness and adaptability of end-to-end (E2E) autonomous driving systems across different domains (e.g., cities, weather conditions). RoCA uses a Gaussian Process (GP) to model the joint probabilistic distribution of ego and agent states, learning a compact codebook of basis tokens that represent diverse driving scenarios. During inference, the GP infers future trajectories based on the similarity between the current scene's token embedding and the learned basis tokens. The GP framework also provides a principled measure of prediction uncertainty, which is then leveraged to weight the training loss, giving more importance to uncertain or difficult predictions. The paper demonstrates that RoCA enhances source domain training, enables effective adaptation to new target domains, and facilitates active learning by selecting the most informative samples for fine-tuning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty of the paper lies in its use of a GP framework to model trajectory distributions in E2E autonomous driving, leveraging this model for regularization during source-domain training, and for uncertainty-aware adaptation in target domains. The approach of learning a codebook of basis tokens to represent driving scenarios is a good approach towards addressing the dataset imbalance issue. Combining basis token learning, trajectory prediction via GP, and uncertainty-weighted loss is a reasonably novel combination. While GP's have been used in other contexts within autonomous driving, this specific application within an E2E pipeline and leveraging the GP's uncertainty measure in this way seems reasonably novel.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical challenge in autonomous driving: domain generalization and adaptation. Current E2E models often struggle to maintain performance when deployed in environments different from their training data. By improving the generalization ability of E2E models, RoCA has the potential to make autonomous driving systems more reliable and safe in diverse real-world scenarios. Also, uncertainty based data sampling using Active learning can significantly reduce annotation efforts. The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating RoCA's effectiveness in various cross-domain scenarios.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Principled approach:** The paper uses a sound probabilistic approach based on Gaussian Processes, providing a theoretical foundation for the method.
    *   **Comprehensive experiments:** The evaluation is thorough, covering various cross-domain scenarios, image degradations, and active learning settings.  The comparisons to existing state-of-the-art methods are well-conducted and demonstrate the superiority of the proposed approach. The ablation studies are also beneficial to see where ROCA achieves the gains.
    *   **Practical benefits:** RoCA offers practical benefits such as improved domain generalization, efficient adaptation, and active learning capabilities.
    *   **Well-written:** The paper is generally well-written and clearly explains the proposed method and experimental results.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational cost:** While the paper mentions computational cost as a minor concern, a thorough computational analysis of the GP component is warranted.
    *   **Scalability:** The scalability of the GP-based approach to very large codebooks could be a concern. While GP inference is tractable, the memory requirements for storing and processing the basis tokens might become problematic with extremely large datasets.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to have a positive impact on the field of autonomous driving. Its ability to address domain shift, efficiently adapt to new environments, and leverage active learning could lead to more robust and cost-effective autonomous driving systems. The techniques introduced could be adopted and extended by other researchers in the field.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a technically sound and well-evaluated method for improving cross-domain performance in end-to-end autonomous driving. The use of Gaussian Processes for regularization, uncertainty quantification, and active learning is a novel and effective approach. However, limitations in computational complexity and the scalability of the GP component keep it from a higher score. While demonstrating compelling results, the reliance on nuScenes also constrains the impact since it is a rather limited data set. The paper's thorough evaluation and practical benefits justify a score of 7, representing a significant contribution that is not paradigm-shifting. The work is a solid incremental improvement upon existing E2E driving systems with a focus on robustness.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### When Large Language Models are Reliable for Judging Empathic Communication
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10150v1)
- **Authors**: Aakriti Kumar, Nalin Poungpeth, Diyi Yang, Erina Farrell, Bruce Lambert, Matthew Groh
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) excel at generating empathic responses in text-based conversations. But, how reliably do they judge the nuances of empathic communication? We investigate this question by comparing how experts, crowdworkers, and LLMs annotate empathic communication across four evaluative frameworks drawn from psychology, natural language processing, and communications applied to 200 real-world conversations where one speaker shares a personal problem and the other offers support. Drawing on 3,150 expert annotations, 2,844 crowd annotations, and 3,150 LLM annotations, we assess inter-rater reliability between these three annotator groups. We find that expert agreement is high but varies across the frameworks' sub-components depending on their clarity, complexity, and subjectivity. We show that expert agreement offers a more informative benchmark for contextualizing LLM performance than standard classification metrics. Across all four frameworks, LLMs consistently approach this expert level benchmark and exceed the reliability of crowdworkers. These results demonstrate how LLMs, when validated on specific tasks with appropriate benchmarks, can support transparency and oversight in emotionally sensitive applications including their use as conversational companions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how reliably large language models (LLMs) can judge empathic communication in text-based conversations, comparing their performance to experts and crowdworkers. The authors assess annotations from these three groups across four established frameworks for evaluating empathy, using real-world conversations where one person shares a problem and the other offers support. They find that while expert agreement varies depending on the framework's clarity and subjectivity, LLMs consistently approach expert-level benchmarks and outperform crowdworkers across the frameworks. The study demonstrates the potential for LLMs to support transparency and oversight in emotionally sensitive applications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The novelty primarily lies in the systematic comparison of LLM performance against human benchmarks *specifically* for the task of *judging* empathic communication, as opposed to *generating* empathic responses, which has received more attention. While there's existing work on LLMs annotating various aspects of text, the focus on empathy judgment across different established frameworks and the rigorous comparison to expert inter-rater reliability are contributions. The introduction of the "Lend an Ear" framework also adds some novelty. The analysis identifying variations across frameworks and specific components within them, is also an important contribution.
*   **Significance:** This work is significant for several reasons:

    *   **Addresses a Key Concern:** It tackles a critical question about the responsible deployment of LLMs in emotionally sensitive applications.  If LLMs are being used as conversational companions or support tools, understanding their ability to reliably assess empathy is crucial for accountability.
    *   **Provides a Strong Methodological Framework:**  The comparative approach using expert inter-rater reliability as the primary benchmark is a strong methodological contribution. It highlights the limitations of relying solely on standard classification metrics (F1-score) when evaluating subjective tasks. The framework itself (expert, crowd, and LLM comparisons) could serve as a template for evaluating other LLM applications involving subjective judgment.
    *   **Practical Implications:**  The finding that LLMs can reliably *judge* empathy in certain contexts has important implications for developing tools that can monitor and assess the quality of LLM-generated responses, offering a pathway to safer and more transparent deployment.
    *   **Highlights Framework Limitations:** The identification of the limitations of specific empathy evaluation frameworks and their components (due to multicollinearity or ambiguity) provides valuable insight for future research and instrument development.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Rigorous Methodology:**  The use of multiple frameworks, multiple annotator groups, and a focus on inter-rater reliability demonstrates a well-designed and carefully executed study.
    *   **Contextualized Evaluation:** The study does not simply treat experts as providing ground truth; it carefully assesses their own inter-rater reliability and uses this to contextualize LLM performance.
    *   **Practical Insights:** Identification of specific framework components where LLMs perform well or poorly is beneficial for targeted improvements.
    *   **Qualitative Analysis:** The inclusion of a qualitative analysis of differing annotations provides a more nuanced understanding of the disagreements among annotator groups.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Conversations:** The study focuses solely on text-based interactions between strangers. The generalizability of the findings to more complex relationships or real-time, multimodal communication settings is unclear.
    *   **Framework Specificity:** The reliance on existing frameworks, while a strength, also limits the study. The frameworks may not fully capture the nuances of empathy, and alternative conceptualizations might yield different results.
    *   **LLM Dependence:** The results are somewhat contingent on the specific LLMs used. While multiple LLMs were used, rapid advancements in the field mean that these models may be quickly superseded by newer, more capable ones.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's rigorous methodology and practical findings could influence research on LLM evaluation and deployment in emotionally sensitive areas. The insights on framework limitations could drive development of more robust empathy assessment tools. This will drive more reliable development of LLMs in social scenarios.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper demonstrates significant novelty and significance by rigorously evaluating LLMs' capacity to judge empathic communication, a critical aspect for responsible AI deployment. The methodological framework, focus on expert reliability, and practical insights contribute meaningfully to the field. While limitations in scope and dependence on specific frameworks exist, the strengths outweigh these weaknesses. It's likely to influence future research on LLM evaluation and applications in emotionally sensitive contexts, promoting greater transparency and accountability.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Can LLMs Generate Good Stories? Insights and Challenges from a Narrative Planning Perspective
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10161v1)
- **Authors**: Yi Wang, Max Kreminski
- **Abstract**: Story generation has been a prominent application of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, understanding LLMs' ability to produce high-quality stories remains limited due to challenges in automatic evaluation methods and the high cost and subjectivity of manual evaluation. Computational narratology offers valuable insights into what constitutes a good story, which has been applied in the symbolic narrative planning approach to story generation. This work aims to deepen the understanding of LLMs' story generation capabilities by using them to solve narrative planning problems. We present a benchmark for evaluating LLMs on narrative planning based on literature examples, focusing on causal soundness, character intentionality, and dramatic conflict. Our experiments show that GPT-4 tier LLMs can generate causally sound stories at small scales, but planning with character intentionality and dramatic conflict remains challenging, requiring LLMs trained with reinforcement learning for complex reasoning. The results offer insights on the scale of stories that LLMs can generate while maintaining quality from different aspects. Our findings also highlight interesting problem solving behaviors and shed lights on challenges and considerations for applying LLM narrative planning in game environments.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "Can LLMs Generate Good Stories? Insights and Challenges from a Narrative Planning Perspective":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate high-quality stories by framing story generation as a narrative planning problem. The authors leverage concepts from computational narratology, specifically focusing on causal soundness, character intentionality, and dramatic conflict, to evaluate LLMs. They create a benchmark using parameterized variations of classic narrative planning examples, encoded with Answer Set Programming (ASP) for automatic validation. Their experiments reveal that GPT-4 tier LLMs can generate causally sound stories at smaller scales, but struggle with intentionality and conflict, suggesting the need for reinforcement learning for more complex reasoning. The paper highlights the challenges and considerations for using LLMs in narrative planning for game environments and suggests the continued role of symbolic narrative planning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its use of a narrative planning perspective to rigorously evaluate LLMs' story generation capabilities. While LLMs have been used for story generation before, this paper departs from simply generating text and instead focuses on validating specific narrative properties (causal soundness, intentionality, conflict) using a computational narratology framework and ASP. The creation of a parameterized benchmark for these properties is also a novel contribution.

*   **Significance:** The work has the potential to be quite significant because it addresses a major problem in LLM story generation: the difficulty of objective evaluation. Existing methods relying on human evaluation are subjective and expensive, while automated metrics often focus on superficial aspects. By adapting narrative planning techniques, the authors provide a more principled and automatable way to assess story quality. The findings on LLMs' strengths and weaknesses regarding different narrative properties are valuable for guiding future research and development in LLM-based story generation. Furthermore, its application in game narrative is extremely relevant, given the push for AI in games.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Rigorous Evaluation Framework:** The use of ASP for automatic validation provides a more objective and scalable evaluation methodology than traditional methods.
    *   **Clear Focus and Scope:** By focusing on specific narrative properties, the paper provides concrete insights into LLMs' capabilities.
    *   **Relevant Benchmark:** The parameterized benchmark allows for systematically exploring the effect of scale and complexity on LLMs' performance.
    *   **Actionable Insights:** The results offer practical guidance for applying LLMs in narrative planning for game environments.
    *   **Integration of Computational Narratology and LLMs:** The paper effectively bridges two distinct research areas.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Model Variety:** The evaluation primarily focuses on GPT-4. While it's a top-tier model, exploring a wider range of LLMs, especially open-source models, could provide a more comprehensive understanding.
    *   **Scope of Narrative Properties:** While causal soundness, intentionality, and conflict are important, other narrative properties (e.g., suspense, character development) are not considered.
    *   **Abstraction of Narrative Planning:** While using narrative planning to assess LLMs is a good framing, some of the richness of true story creation is lost by reducing it to achieving goals through planning with pre-defined actions. This makes the work a validation benchmark for LLMs, but perhaps less so a path towards actual creative story generation.
    *   **Simplifications for ASP Encoding:** The paper makes some simplifying assumptions (e.g., limited types of intentions) for the ASP encoding, which might limit the expressiveness of the framework and impact the generalizability of the results.
    *   **Reliance on Symbolic Approach:** The results and conclusions are contingent on the chosen formalization for narrative planning. It might be argued that LLMs' way of "understanding" stories is fundamentally different, and the formalization might not capture relevant aspects of storytelling.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in LLM-based story generation by providing a more rigorous evaluation methodology and identifying specific areas where LLMs need improvement. It could also spur further research on integrating symbolic narrative planning techniques with LLMs. The open sourcing of the code and data will assist in reproducibility.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a solid and significant contribution, but it's not groundbreaking. The use of narrative planning and ASP is clever and provides a valuable new lens for evaluating LLMs. The benchmark and experimental results are insightful and actionable. However, the limitations regarding model variety, scope of narrative properties, and simplifying assumptions prevent it from being a truly exceptional contribution. I will also temper the score because the narrative planning formulation is more of a validation task than a generative methodology. Also, the success on the "easy" causal domain is to be expected, thus the paper really shines from the observations about intentional and conflicting narrative.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### SPARKE: Scalable Prompt-Aware Diversity Guidance in Diffusion Models via RKE Score
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10173v1)
- **Authors**: Mohammad Jalali, Haoyu Lei, Amin Gohari, Farzan Farnia
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have demonstrated remarkable success in high-fidelity image synthesis and prompt-guided generative modeling. However, ensuring adequate diversity in generated samples of prompt-guided diffusion models remains a challenge, particularly when the prompts span a broad semantic spectrum and the diversity of generated data needs to be evaluated in a prompt-aware fashion across semantically similar prompts. Recent methods have introduced guidance via diversity measures to encourage more varied generations. In this work, we extend the diversity measure-based approaches by proposing the Scalable Prompt-Aware R\'eny Kernel Entropy Diversity Guidance (SPARKE) method for prompt-aware diversity guidance. SPARKE utilizes conditional entropy for diversity guidance, which dynamically conditions diversity measurement on similar prompts and enables prompt-aware diversity control. While the entropy-based guidance approach enhances prompt-aware diversity, its reliance on the matrix-based entropy scores poses computational challenges in large-scale generation settings. To address this, we focus on the special case of Conditional latent RKE Score Guidance, reducing entropy computation and gradient-based optimization complexity from the $O(n^3)$ of general entropy measures to $O(n)$. The reduced computational complexity allows for diversity-guided sampling over potentially thousands of generation rounds on different prompts. We numerically test the SPARKE method on several text-to-image diffusion models, demonstrating that the proposed method improves the prompt-aware diversity of the generated data without incurring significant computational costs. We release our code on the project page: https://mjalali.github.io/SPARKE
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SPARKE, a Scalable Prompt-Aware Rényi Kernel Entropy Diversity Guidance method for improving diversity in diffusion models, especially in prompt-guided generation. SPARKE uses conditional entropy to dynamically condition diversity measurements on similar prompts, enabling prompt-aware diversity control. To address computational challenges associated with entropy-based guidance (O(n^3) complexity), the authors focus on the Conditional latent RKE Score Guidance, which reduces complexity to O(n), making it scalable for large-scale generation. The method is tested on text-to-image diffusion models, demonstrating improved prompt-aware diversity without significant computational overhead. The code is publicly released.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using conditional entropy for prompt-aware diversity guidance is a valuable extension to existing diversity measure-based approaches. By dynamically conditioning on similar prompts, it addresses a limitation of methods that rely on fixed prompt clusters or unconditional diversity measures. The specific application of the Rényi Kernel Entropy (RKE) score to reduce computational complexity is also a significant contribution, enabling scalability. While the individual components (conditional entropy, RKE) are not entirely new, their combination and application to this specific problem offer novelty.
*   **Significance:** Enhancing diversity in generative models, especially in prompt-guided scenarios, is important for fairness, generalization, and avoiding mode collapse. SPARKE tackles a practical challenge in scaling diversity guidance to large datasets and complex prompts. The reduced computational complexity makes it more accessible and applicable to real-world generative tasks. The empirical results, demonstrating improved diversity without sacrificing fidelity, further highlight the significance.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   Addresses a key challenge in prompt-guided diffusion models: ensuring adequate diversity while considering prompt variability.
    *   Introduces a scalable solution via the Conditional latent RKE Score Guidance, reducing computational complexity from O(n³) to O(n).
    *   Empirically validated on multiple text-to-image diffusion models, demonstrating improved prompt-aware diversity and efficiency.
    *   Provides a well-documented and publicly released code implementation.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the computational complexity is reduced, the O(n) optimization is still a gradient-based method, so it requires careful parameter tuning and the optimization could be unstable.
    *   The evaluation primarily focuses on text-to-image generation. Extending the method and evaluating its performance on other modalities (e.g., video, audio) would further demonstrate its generalizability.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more extensive comparison with other diversity-enhancing techniques, especially those based on reinforcement learning or adversarial training.
    *   Although it achieves a better trade-off between diversity and fidelity, the reported scores are still relatively low, indicating that a better method should achieve a balance.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence research in diversity-aware generative modeling, particularly in prompt-guided settings. The proposed SPARKE method provides a practical and scalable approach for enhancing diversity, which can be adopted and extended by other researchers and practitioners. It may also inspire new techniques for optimizing diversity guidance in diffusion models.
*   **Rigorous rationale for the score:**

    *   The paper presents a novel combination of existing techniques, addressing a specific and practically relevant problem in diffusion models.
    *   The computational improvements are significant, enabling scalability to larger datasets and more complex prompts.
    *   The empirical results demonstrate clear improvements in prompt-aware diversity without major fidelity trade-offs.
    *   While the scope of the evaluation is somewhat limited (primarily text-to-image generation), the findings are promising and warrant further investigation in other modalities.
    *   However, the overall quality (diversity, fidelity) is still relatively low, requiring future improvement.
