### AURA: A Multi-Agent Intelligence Framework for Knowledge-Enhanced Cyber Threat Attribution
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10175v1)
- **Authors**: Nanda Rani, Sandeep Kumar Shukla
- **Abstract**: Effective attribution of Advanced Persistent Threats (APTs) increasingly hinges on the ability to correlate behavioral patterns and reason over complex, varied threat intelligence artifacts. We present AURA (Attribution Using Retrieval-Augmented Agents), a multi-agent, knowledge-enhanced framework for automated and interpretable APT attribution. AURA ingests diverse threat data including Tactics, Techniques, and Procedures (TTPs), Indicators of Compromise (IoCs), malware details, adversarial tools, and temporal information, which are processed through a network of collaborative agents. These agents are designed for intelligent query rewriting, context-enriched retrieval from structured threat knowledge bases, and natural language justification of attribution decisions. By combining Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) with Large Language Models (LLMs), AURA enables contextual linking of threat behaviors to known APT groups and supports traceable reasoning across multiple attack phases. Experiments on recent APT campaigns demonstrate AURA's high attribution consistency, expert-aligned justifications, and scalability. This work establishes AURA as a promising direction for advancing transparent, data-driven, and scalable threat attribution using multi-agent intelligence.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AURA, a multi-agent intelligence framework for knowledge-enhanced cyber threat attribution. AURA leverages Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) with Large Language Models (LLMs) in a modular, agent-based architecture. The framework processes diverse threat data (TTPs, IoCs, etc.) through collaborative agents that handle query rewriting, context-enriched retrieval, and justification generation. The paper demonstrates AURA's effectiveness on real-world APT campaigns, showing high attribution consistency, expert-aligned justifications, and scalability. The system achieves impressive accuracy in attributing cyber threats, especially in complex and ambiguous scenarios, and generates human-readable justifications. The authors also explore the benefits of combining structured threat data with LLMs for improved accuracy and interpretability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Architecture:** The multi-agent RAG-based architecture for cyber threat attribution is a key strength. It is a well-designed and thoughtful application of LLMs to a challenging cybersecurity problem. The modularity of AURA allows for easier debugging, maintenance, and future expansion.
*   **Contextual Reasoning & Explainability:** The paper's emphasis on providing interpretable outputs with natural language justifications is significant. This addresses a major limitation of previous ML/NLP-based attribution methods, which often lack transparency and trustworthiness. The use of a decision agent to filter irrelevant context further enhances accuracy.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper conducts a thorough evaluation using real-world APT campaign datasets and assesses the quality of generated justifications through both automated metrics and LLM-as-judge methods. This provides strong evidence for the framework's effectiveness.
*   **Real-World Case Study:** The case study provides a tangible example of AURA's capabilities in real-world scenarios, showcasing its ability to synthesize technical indicators with contextual signals.
*   **Handles Ambiguity:** The paper acknowledges and addresses the challenges posed by overlapping modus operandi among threat actors, demonstrating AURA's ability to provide nuanced attribution even in ambiguous situations.
*   **Focus on Data Diversity:** The paper clearly addresses the value of integrating diverse data sources (TTPs, IOCs, and malware behavior), which is critical in threat attribution and is superior to methods based on a single source.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Dataset Size:** The paper acknowledges that the evaluation is constrained by the relatively small test set size (30 threat reports). Although efforts were made to exclude potentially biased reports, a larger and more diverse dataset would strengthen the results and improve generalizability.
*   **Black-box LLM Dependence:** While using black-box LLMs demonstrates AURA's capabilities, it also raises concerns about transparency and reproducibility. Evaluating AURA with open-source LLMs would promote greater accessibility and trust.
*   **Justification Weighting:** The lack of evidence weighting in the generated justifications is a potential weakness. Highlighting the most important evidence would further enhance the usefulness of the explanations.
*   **Limited Scope:** While comprehensive, the work focuses on attribution at the threat group and nation level. Future work could explore finer-grained attribution (e.g., individual actors, specific campaigns).

**Novelty & Significance:**

The use of a modular multi-agent system combined with RAG to perform knowledge-enhanced cyber threat attribution has significance because it addresses shortcomings in existing methods. Prior approaches often lacked contextual awareness, explainability, or the ability to integrate diverse data sources. AURA's ability to generate understandable justifications and reason over diverse inputs represents a substantial step forward.
Also the paper is well-written and easy to follow.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of 8/10.

*   The paper presents a novel and well-engineered architecture for cyber threat attribution that addresses significant limitations in the field. The modular, RAG-based approach allows for improvements in context awareness and transparency which is a problem in earlier approaches.
*   It offers comprehensive evaluation and demonstrated strong performance, the relatively small dataset size and dependence on black-box LLMs limit the impact and generalizability of the findings. A larger and more representative evaluation, including a wider array of threat landscapes, attribution cases, and the addition of open-source LLMs would significantly increase the score.
* Overall, the paper makes a valuable contribution and offers a promising direction for future research in cyber threat attribution, and can be regarded as a high-quality research paper.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Geometric Regularity in Deterministic Sampling of Diffusion-based Generative Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10177v1)
- **Authors**: Defang Chen, Zhenyu Zhou, Can Wang, Siwei Lyu
- **Abstract**: Diffusion-based generative models employ stochastic differential equations (SDEs) and their equivalent probability flow ordinary differential equations (ODEs) to establish a smooth transformation between complex high-dimensional data distributions and tractable prior distributions. In this paper, we reveal a striking geometric regularity in the deterministic sampling dynamics: each simulated sampling trajectory lies within an extremely low-dimensional subspace, and all trajectories exhibit an almost identical ''boomerang'' shape, regardless of the model architecture, applied conditions, or generated content. We characterize several intriguing properties of these trajectories, particularly under closed-form solutions based on kernel-estimated data modeling. We also demonstrate a practical application of the discovered trajectory regularity by proposing a dynamic programming-based scheme to better align the sampling time schedule with the underlying trajectory structure. This simple strategy requires minimal modification to existing ODE-based numerical solvers, incurs negligible computational overhead, and achieves superior image generation performance, especially in regions with only $5 \sim 10$ function evaluations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Geometric Regularity in Deterministic Sampling of Diffusion-based Generative Models" explores the geometric properties of deterministic sampling trajectories in diffusion models. The authors observe that these trajectories, regardless of the generated content or model architecture, exhibit a consistent "boomerang" shape and lie within a low-dimensional subspace. They analyze these trajectories using concepts from differential geometry (Frenet-Serret formulas) and connect the sampling dynamics to kernel density estimation (KDE). The paper proposes a dynamic programming-based method (GITS - Geometry Inspired Time Scheduling) to optimize the sampling time schedule based on the identified geometric regularity. Experimental results demonstrate that GITS improves image generation performance, especially with a small number of function evaluations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in identifying and characterizing the geometric regularity of deterministic sampling trajectories.  Prior work has touched upon the denoising trajectory but the explicit link to differential geometry and low dimensionality is a relatively fresh perspective. The connection to kernel density estimation and the subsequent analysis of stepwise rotation and monotone likelihood increase provide new insights into the sampling process. The GITS algorithm, while not groundbreaking in its use of dynamic programming, is a practical application of the discovered regularity and contributes to accelerated sampling. While other works propose to optimize the time schedules for diffusion model sampling, GITS approach is particularly unique that they leverage the geometry aspects of the underlying ODE trajectory for optimization, which is interesting and novel.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from several factors:

    *   **Understanding Diffusion Dynamics:** The paper offers a deeper understanding of how diffusion models work, beyond the typical stochastic differential equation (SDE) or ordinary differential equation (ODE) formulations.  The geometric perspective provides a more intuitive explanation of the sampling process.
    *   **Practical Improvement:** The GITS algorithm offers a practical method for improving the efficiency of diffusion model sampling, a crucial aspect given the computational cost of these models. The fact that it performs well with only a few function evaluations is especially valuable.
    *   **Unification of Heuristics:** The paper provides a theoretical grounding for existing heuristics related to time schedule design, explaining why certain schedules work well in practice.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Empirical Validation:** The paper provides thorough empirical evidence supporting its claims about geometric regularity, with experiments on various datasets and model architectures.
    *   **Theoretical Depth:** The analysis using differential geometry and KDE provides a solid theoretical foundation for the observations.
    *   **Clear and Well-Structured:** The paper is well-written and logically structured, making it relatively easy to follow the arguments.
    *   **Practicality:** The GITS algorithm is simple to implement and offers a tangible improvement in sampling efficiency.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** While the GITS algorithm shows promise, the performance gains might be limited to specific types of models or datasets. More extensive benchmarking and analysis of the limitations would be beneficial.
    *   **Overclaim:** Trajectories exhibit a "similar" shape is rather subjective and qualitative than quantitative, therefore makes it difficult to evaluate the method.
    *   **Missing details on time schedule search**: The method to select the time schedule for the GITS method is rather unclear.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in diffusion models by:

    *   Inspiring more work on the geometric aspects of diffusion processes.
    *   Motivating the development of new sampling algorithms that exploit the low-dimensional structure of trajectories.
    *   Providing a framework for understanding and optimizing time schedules in diffusion models.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and interesting perspective on diffusion models by highlighting the geometric regularity of sampling trajectories. The connection to KDE and differential geometry is valuable, and the GITS algorithm demonstrates a practical application of these insights. However, the limited scope and the simplicity of GITS (in its use of dynamic programming) prevent a higher score. The impact of this work is primarily on deepening our understanding of diffusion model dynamics rather than introducing a disruptive innovation in sampling methods. But still, GITS provides useful insights for future research, hence it worth 7 for the method’s novelty and practicality.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Scalable Non-Equivariant 3D Molecule Generation via Rotational Alignment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10186v1)
- **Authors**: Yuhui Ding, Thomas Hofmann
- **Abstract**: Equivariant diffusion models have achieved impressive performance in 3D molecule generation. These models incorporate Euclidean symmetries of 3D molecules by utilizing an SE(3)-equivariant denoising network. However, specialized equivariant architectures limit the scalability and efficiency of diffusion models. In this paper, we propose an approach that relaxes such equivariance constraints. Specifically, our approach learns a sample-dependent SO(3) transformation for each molecule to construct an aligned latent space. A non-equivariant diffusion model is then trained over the aligned representations. Experimental results demonstrate that our approach performs significantly better than previously reported non-equivariant models. It yields sample quality comparable to state-of-the-art equivariant diffusion models and offers improved training and sampling efficiency. Our code is available at https://github.com/skeletondyh/RADM
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Scalable Non-Equivariant 3D Molecule Generation via Rotational Alignment":

**Summary:**

This paper addresses the problem of generating 3D molecules using diffusion models. Current state-of-the-art approaches rely on equivariant diffusion models, which utilize specialized, computationally expensive architectures to respect the Euclidean symmetries (SE(3)) of molecules. The authors propose a method to relax these equivariance constraints, arguing that equivariance is not strictly necessary for effective molecular generation.  Their approach, Rotationally Aligned Diffusion Model (RADM), learns a sample-dependent SO(3) transformation to align molecules into a consistent latent space. A non-equivariant diffusion model is then trained on these aligned representations. This allows the use of simpler, more efficient architectures like transformers (DiT) as the denoising network.  Experimental results show RADM achieves performance comparable to state-of-the-art equivariant models while offering improved training and sampling efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of aligning molecules before applying a non-equivariant diffusion model has some novelty.  While alignment isn't entirely new in other 3D domains, applying it to molecular generation within a diffusion framework and in a completely unsupervised manner is a worthwhile exploration. The method for learning the alignment via an autoencoder with a separate rotation prediction network is a sound technical contribution.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper lies in its potential to simplify and scale up molecular generation. Equivariant architectures are complex and often less efficient. By demonstrating that near state-of-the-art results can be achieved *without* strict equivariance, the paper opens the door to using more standard and efficient deep learning building blocks (e.g., transformers) in this domain. The improved efficiency could enable the generation of larger molecules and datasets, which is crucial for drug discovery applications. The paper makes the generative models much scalable due to using transformers, which has been difficult due to use of equivariant models.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong empirical results:** The experimental results on QM9 and GEOM-Drugs are convincing. The authors carefully compare against a range of baselines, including state-of-the-art equivariant and non-equivariant models. Their results are better than other non-equivariant models.

    *   **Clear writing and methodology:** The paper is well-written and the methodology is clearly explained. The authors justify their design choices and provide implementation details that facilitate reproducibility. The ablation study is useful in validating the importance of the alignment component.
    *   **Efficiency Gains:** RADM demonstrates improved training and sampling speed compared to equivariant models, highlighting the practical benefits of their approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited theoretical justification for why equivariance is not necessary:** While the authors argue that equivariance is not strictly necessary, they do not offer a strong theoretical explanation for why their alignment strategy works so well, and how it effectively compensates for the lack of equivariance. A more rigorous analysis of this aspect would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Reliance on an autoencoder:** The performance of RADM is inherently tied to the effectiveness of the autoencoder in creating a meaningful latent space. The paper acknowledges this but does not delve deeply into the limitations or potential issues arising from the autoencoder bottleneck. The results obtained when using the same network as the diffusion models is not impressive.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Alignment Space**: the approach to alignment is well grounded in mathematical terms, the models could also be explored from how the generated alignment relate to important aspects of the chemical properties of the compounds.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the direction of research in molecular generation. If the proposed approach can be further refined and scaled, it could become a viable alternative to equivariant models, especially in settings where computational resources are limited. The approach may influence how 3D structure is modeled.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**
The paper presents a novel and empirically strong approach to molecular generation that challenges the prevailing reliance on equivariant models. The improvements in efficiency and scalability are significant. However, the lack of a deeper theoretical justification for why the alignment strategy works and the dependence on an autoencoder limit the overall impact. While it could be potentially very influential, there are still fundamental aspects that could improve performance and provide the reader with a deeper understanding of the method. Therefore, a score of 7 reflects a solid contribution with clear potential for future development.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Prompt Variability Effects On LLM Code Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10204v1)
- **Authors**: Andrei Paleyes, Radzim Sendyka, Diana Robinson, Christian Cabrera, Neil D. Lawrence
- **Abstract**: Code generation is one of the most active areas of application of Large Language Models (LLMs). While LLMs lower barriers to writing code and accelerate development process, the overall quality of generated programs depends on the quality of given prompts. Specifically, functionality and quality of generated code can be sensitive to user's background and familiarity with software development. It is therefore important to quantify LLM's sensitivity to variations in the input. To this end we propose a synthetic evaluation pipeline for code generation with LLMs, as well as a systematic persona-based evaluation approach to expose qualitative differences of LLM responses dependent on prospective user background. Both proposed methods are completely independent from specific programming tasks and LLMs, and thus are widely applicable. We provide experimental evidence illustrating utility of our methods and share our code for the benefit of the community.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the sensitivity of Large Language Models (LLMs) to variations in prompts when generating code. The authors propose two evaluation methods: (1) a synthetic evaluation pipeline that introduces artificial changes (typos, synonyms, paraphrasing) to prompts and measures the resulting code similarity, and (2) a persona-based evaluation where different personas (defined by varying levels of programming experience) generate prompts for the same coding task, and the resulting code is analyzed for qualitative differences.  They test these methods across different LLMs and datasets, finding that LLMs are sensitive to prompt variations, particularly typos, and that user background influences the generated code. The code and datasets are publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a relevant and important problem:** The paper directly tackles a crucial issue: the reliability of LLM-generated code given the variability in how users formulate prompts. This is significant as LLMs become more widely used by individuals with varying levels of technical expertise.
    *   **Proposes two distinct evaluation methods:** The synthetic and persona-based approaches offer complementary ways to analyze prompt sensitivity. The synthetic method allows for controlled experimentation, while the persona-based method introduces a more realistic user context.
    *   **Systematic experimentation:** The paper presents well-defined experimental setups, varying prompt augmentation strategies and using multiple LLMs and datasets.
    *   **Openly available resources:** Sharing the code and datasets promotes reproducibility and allows other researchers to build on their work.
    *   **Identifies potential directions for improvement:** The paper's findings suggest avenues for improving code LLMs, such as incorporating user guidance or prompt regularization techniques. The persona-based approach highlights the need for LLMs to adapt to the user's skill level.
* **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited scope of personas:** The persona-based evaluation, while insightful, only considers four personas. A more comprehensive study with a wider range of backgrounds and experiences would strengthen the findings.
    *   **Qualitative analysis:** The persona-based analysis is largely qualitative and observational. While the use of linguistic visual analytics is a plus, more rigorous quantitative measures to compare the code generated under different personas would improve the evaluation's objectivity.
    *   **Lack of ground truth for persona-based code:** The paper doesn't explicitly assess the correctness of code generated using persona prompts, focusing instead on qualitative differences. This makes it hard to determine if the persona-influenced prompts are actually better or worse.
    *   **Synthetic augmentations might not fully represent real-world variations:** Although useful for initial benchmarking, typos, synonyms, and paraphrasing might not fully capture the nuances of human-generated prompts.
    *   **Small LeetCode New size:** Although using a new LeetCode data set is a good step, the small size makes broad conclusions questionable.
* **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The combination of synthetic prompt manipulation and persona-based evaluation for analyzing LLM code generation is relatively novel.  While individual aspects have been explored before, the systematic combination is a valuable contribution.
    *   The work extends beyond just evaluating correctness; it delves into how prompts influence the *style* and *granularity* of generated code, opening up new research directions related to usability and user experience.
    *   The public availability of the code and datasets significantly enhances the impact and facilitates follow-up research.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper addresses a relevant problem with a novel approach using sound methodology. The inclusion of different LLMs and datasets strengthens the validity of results. The weaknesses primarily lie in the limited scope of the persona-based study and the largely qualitative analysis. However, the paper lays a solid foundation for future research in this area. The open-sourcing of resources is a significant plus.
