    *   **Clarity in Implementation Details:** Some implementation details, such as the specific choice of Fourier transform and the handling of boundary conditions in spectral space, could be clarified further.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more robust and accurate deep learning-based PDE solvers. The spectral adjustment technique could be adopted and extended by other researchers in the field. The analysis of frequency spectra and their impact on simulation accuracy provides valuable insights for future research.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, I assign a score of **7.5**.

Here's the rationale:
* The use of diffusion models in PDE solvers is an area of active research, as indicated by the cited work of Lippe et al. 2024. The present paper's novelty lies in the introduction of spectral control, which is a substantial improvement.
* The paper is grounded in a clear understanding of the spectral characteristics of PDEs and the limitations of existing methods.
* The methodology is technically sound, and the new v-prediction technique is well-integrated.
* The experimental results demonstrate clear improvements on a challenging problem, and the spectral analysis provides valuable insights.
* The weaknesses (scope of PDEs, hyperparameter sensitivity, lack of theoretical justification) are limitations but do not fundamentally undermine the value of the contribution. They represent areas for future research and refinement.

Therefore, while not a groundbreaking, paradigm-shifting contribution (which would warrant a higher score), the paper represents a solid and significant advancement in the field of deep learning-based PDE solvers. The combination of spectral adjustment and blurring v-prediction is effective in improving simulation accuracy and stability.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Inferring Adjective Hypernyms with Language Models to Increase the Connectivity of Open English Wordnet
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10715v1)
- **Authors**: Lorenzo Augello, John P. McCrae
- **Abstract**: Open English Wordnet is a key resource published in OntoLex-lemon as part of the linguistic linked open data cloud. There are, however, many links missing in the resource, and in this paper, we look at how we can establish hypernymy between adjectives. We present a theoretical discussion of the hypernymy relation and how it differs for adjectives in contrast to nouns and verbs. We develop a new resource for adjective hypernymy and fine-tune large language models to predict adjective hypernymy, showing that the methodology of TaxoLLaMa can be adapted to this task.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper addresses the under-explored area of adjective hypernymy within lexical semantics. It begins by highlighting the limitations of Open English Wordnet (OEWN) concerning adjective connectivity and argues for the importance of establishing hypernymy relations between adjectives.  The authors present a theoretical framework for adjective hypernymy, emphasizing the need for sense disambiguation due to polysemy and context dependence. They create a gold-standard English dataset of hyponym-hypernym adjective pairs, leveraging existing lexical resources from languages like Polish and Dutch that explicitly model adjectival hierarchies. The dataset is constructed with careful human annotation and validation, addressing semantic ambiguity using a substitution-based approach. The authors then evaluate the ability of large language models (TaxoLLaMa and SmolLM-360M-Instruct) to recognize and interpret adjective hypernymy, comparing their performance before and after fine-tuning on the created dataset. Results show that fine-tuning significantly improves performance, particularly with a synonym-augmented version of the dataset. The paper emphasizes the role of word sense disambiguation and the benefits of allowing multiple valid hypernyms during training.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in directly tackling the largely ignored area of adjective hypernymy. While hypernymy extraction for nouns and verbs has been extensively studied, this paper provides a dedicated focus on adjectives, a category with unique semantic challenges like gradability and context-dependence. The creation of a new gold-standard dataset tailored to adjective hypernymy is also a significant contribution. The paper also introduces a novel approach to leveraging existing wordnets from other languages in order to bootstrap hypernym construction.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work is multi-faceted. First, it addresses a clear gap in existing lexical resources and NLP research. By focusing on adjectives, the paper has the potential to increase the connectivity and utility of resources like OEWN. Second, the theoretical framework provides a foundation for future research on adjective semantics. Third, the dataset serves as a valuable benchmark for evaluating language models' abilities to capture nuanced semantic relations between adjectives. Finally, demonstrating that existing models can be successfully fine-tuned on this dataset suggests that they can be made more sensitive to the complexities of adjective semantics. This can impact downstream tasks like text understanding and generation.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined theoretical framework for adjective hypernymy.
    *   Rigorous dataset construction methodology with human annotation and validation.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation of language models with zero-shot and fine-tuning experiments.
    *   Insightful analysis of the results, highlighting the importance of sense disambiguation and multiple hypernyms.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dataset Size:** The dataset is relatively small. While the authors acknowledge this limitation, a larger dataset would improve the robustness of the evaluation and the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Limited Model Evaluation:** The paper only evaluates two specific language models. While the choice of TaxoLLaMa is justifiable given its WordNet training, a broader range of models could provide more comprehensive insights. A comparison with other fine-tuning methods may be also beneficial.
    *   **English-Centric:** The focus on English might limit the generalizability of the findings to other languages. Although the seed adjective pairs were sourced from different languages, the final dataset is exclusively in English.
    *   **Inter-Annotator Agreement:** While the authors reported "moderate" agreement levels, it may be useful to dig deeper into reasons of disagreements and explore methods to improve consistency (e.g., better annotation guidelines or more specialized annotators).

*   **Justification:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of lexical semantics and natural language processing by addressing the understudied area of adjective hypernymy. The theoretical framework and dataset creation represent a significant effort, and the empirical evaluation provides useful insights into language model capabilities. While the limitations related to dataset size and model evaluation are present, they don't significantly detract from the overall contribution. The work is well-motivated and rigorously conducted. It opens up new avenues for research in adjective semantics and language model evaluation.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### PREMISE: Scalable and Strategic Prompt Optimization for Efficient Mathematical Reasoning in Large Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10716v1)
- **Authors**: Ye Yu, Yaoning Yu, Haohan Wang
- **Abstract**: Large reasoning models (LRMs) such as Claude 3.7 Sonnet and OpenAI o1 achieve strong performance on mathematical benchmarks using lengthy chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning, but the resulting traces are often unnecessarily verbose. This inflates token usage and cost, limiting deployment in latency-sensitive or API-constrained settings. We introduce PREMISE (PRompt-based Efficient Mathematical Inference with Strategic Evaluation), a prompt-only framework that reduces reasoning overhead without modifying model weights. PREMISE combines trace-level diagnostics with gradient-inspired prompt optimization to minimize redundant computation while preserving answer accuracy. The approach jointly optimizes brevity and correctness through a multi-objective textual search that balances token length and answer validity. Unlike prior work, PREMISE runs in a single-pass black-box interface, so it can be applied directly to commercial LLMs. On GSM8K, SVAMP, and Math500 we match or exceed baseline accuracy ($96\%\rightarrow96\%$ with Claude, $91\%\rightarrow92\%$ with Gemini) while reducing reasoning tokens by up to $87.5\%$ and cutting dollar cost by $69$--$82\%$. These results show that prompt-level optimization is a practical and scalable path to efficient LRM inference without compromising reasoning quality.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PREMISE, a prompt-only framework for improving the efficiency of large reasoning models (LRMs) on mathematical tasks. PREMISE focuses on reducing the computational overhead (token usage and cost) associated with the often verbose Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning traces generated by these models. The framework uses trace-level diagnostics (identifying overthinking and underthinking) combined with gradient-based prompt optimization to encourage more concise and accurate reasoning. PREMISE operates entirely within a single-pass, black-box interface, making it applicable to commercial LLMs like Claude, GPT, and Gemini.  The authors evaluate PREMISE on GSM8K, SVAMP, and Math500 datasets, demonstrating that it can match or exceed baseline accuracy while significantly reducing token usage and cost.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a relevant and practical problem: the inefficiency of CoT prompting in large language models, especially concerning token usage and cost in deployed applications.  The primary strength of the paper lies in its practical applicability. The fact that PREMISE is a prompt-only framework, requiring no model fine-tuning or access to model weights, makes it immediately useful for commercial LLM APIs.

*Novelty:*  While prompt engineering and optimization are not new, the combination of trace-level diagnostics *with* gradient-based optimization for prompt refinement is a more sophisticated approach than simple heuristic or static length constraints. The definitions of overthinking and underthinking, although conceptually straightforward, provide a more structured way to approach the problem of inefficient reasoning.  It stands out as the first framework that integrates formal trace-level reasoning metrics with dynamic optimization and prompt-level control in a black-box setting.

*Significance:* The results are promising. The reported reductions in token usage (up to 87.5%) and cost (69-82%) without sacrificing accuracy could have a significant impact on the real-world deployment of LRMs, particularly in latency-sensitive or resource-constrained scenarios. The framework provides a path to using CoT prompting effectively in cost-sensitive commercial applications.

*Weaknesses:* The paper lacks a deeper theoretical analysis of why PREMISE works. While the empirical results are compelling, a better understanding of the interaction between the prompt optimization and the model's internal reasoning process would strengthen the contribution.  The reliance on gradient-based prompt optimization also raises concerns about robustness. Changes to the underlying model (e.g., a new version of Claude or GPT) could potentially invalidate the optimized prompts, requiring re-optimization. The paper could be improved by including a discussion of prompt robustness and potential strategies for adapting prompts to new model versions. The discussion of the OpenAI result (increased token usage) feels a bit hand-wavey and could be clarified by more rigorous experimentation in the main result section. It seems the model is not adhering to concise reasoning, and this point would be better tested/explained with the same ablation strategy in the single model section. Furthermore, the improvements on the accuracy metric should be highlighted further as beneficial results.

*Potential Influence:*  The paper is likely to influence future research on efficient reasoning in LLMs. It demonstrates the potential of prompt-level optimization for addressing the inefficiency of CoT prompting, and it provides a framework that can be extended and adapted to other reasoning tasks and model architectures. The introduced metrics could become a standard way to evaluate and compare the efficiency of different reasoning methods.

**Rigorous Rationale:**
The paper is a practical and innovative approach to improving the efficiency of LRM based reasoning. The combination of black-box optimization, with diagnostics provides tangible results and an approach for further refinement. However, some key points could be explored in more detail for greater understanding, and there are a few areas that could benefit from some more rigorous experimentation. Considering the paper's practicality, novelty, and potential impact, but taking into account the weaknesses noted above, a score of 7 is appropriate.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### TaxoAdapt: Aligning LLM-Based Multidimensional Taxonomy Construction to Evolving Research Corpora
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10737v1)
- **Authors**: Priyanka Kargupta, Nan Zhang, Yunyi Zhang, Rui Zhang, Prasenjit Mitra, Jiawei Han
- **Abstract**: The rapid evolution of scientific fields introduces challenges in organizing and retrieving scientific literature. While expert-curated taxonomies have traditionally addressed this need, the process is time-consuming and expensive. Furthermore, recent automatic taxonomy construction methods either (1) over-rely on a specific corpus, sacrificing generalizability, or (2) depend heavily on the general knowledge of large language models (LLMs) contained within their pre-training datasets, often overlooking the dynamic nature of evolving scientific domains. Additionally, these approaches fail to account for the multi-faceted nature of scientific literature, where a single research paper may contribute to multiple dimensions (e.g., methodology, new tasks, evaluation metrics, benchmarks). To address these gaps, we propose TaxoAdapt, a framework that dynamically adapts an LLM-generated taxonomy to a given corpus across multiple dimensions. TaxoAdapt performs iterative hierarchical classification, expanding both the taxonomy width and depth based on corpus' topical distribution. We demonstrate its state-of-the-art performance across a diverse set of computer science conferences over the years to showcase its ability to structure and capture the evolution of scientific fields. As a multidimensional method, TaxoAdapt generates taxonomies that are 26.51% more granularity-preserving and 50.41% more coherent than the most competitive baselines judged by LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "TaxoAdapt: Aligning LLM-Based Multidimensional Taxonomy Construction to Evolving Research Corpora":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TaxoAdapt, a framework designed to dynamically adapt LLM-generated taxonomies to specific corpora across multiple dimensions (tasks, methods, datasets, etc.). Addressing the limitations of existing taxonomy construction methods (corpus-driven and LLM-based), TaxoAdapt performs iterative hierarchical classification, expanding taxonomy width and depth based on the topical distribution of a given corpus. The paper demonstrates state-of-the-art performance in structuring and capturing the evolution of scientific fields using computer science conferences as examples. A key feature is its multidimensional approach, resulting in taxonomies that are claimed to be more granularity-preserving and coherent than baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty stems from its holistic approach to taxonomy construction. It explicitly addresses the *multidimensionality* of scientific research and the *dynamic nature* of research corpora, aspects often overlooked by existing methods. The iterative, classification-driven approach to taxonomy adaptation is also a novel contribution. However, the individual components, such as the LLM prompting techniques and clustering algorithms, are not entirely novel in themselves. The real novelty lies in the clever *integration* of these components and the application to the specific problem of scientific taxonomy construction.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work rests on its potential to improve the organization and retrieval of scientific literature. A dynamically adapting, multidimensional taxonomy can facilitate better search engines, knowledge discovery, and a deeper understanding of evolving research trends. This is particularly important in rapidly developing fields where manual curation of taxonomies struggles to keep pace. The reported improvements in granularity and coherence relative to baselines support the claim of improved organization. The adaptability of the method to different corpora and dimensions is a strength, demonstrating the potential for broad applicability.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clearly defined problem and motivation.
    *   Well-structured framework with a logical flow.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation with a diverse set of computer science conferences and carefully designed metrics.
    *   Addresses a gap in the existing literature by considering multidimensionality and dynamic evolution.
    *   Code and dataset are released, promoting reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Reliance on LLMs: While a strength, the dependence on LLMs and their pre-existing knowledge is also a potential weakness. The quality of the taxonomy is inherently tied to the performance of the LLMs. (Although the research attempts to mitigate this by knowledge grounding.)
    *   Limited exploration of different LLMs: The primary evaluation involves one open source, and a closed source LLM (and its performance, as always, can fluctuate, and has fluctuations over time) and it would strengthen the results to test with a range of LLMs.
    *   Specific domain focus: The experiments primarily focus on computer science. While the authors argue the framework is general, more evidence from other scientific domains would strengthen the claims.
    *   The improvement in real-world domain classification (Section G) needs to be tested further for hallucination.

*   **Impact:** If successful, TaxoAdapt can influence the way scientific knowledge is organized and accessed, leading to more efficient research and discovery. The work also encourages a more holistic view of scientific literature, recognizing the multiple facets of research papers.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, the paper presents a *meaningful contribution* to the field. The framework is well-designed, the experimental results are promising, and the approach addresses an important challenge. However, the reliance on LLMs and the specific domain focus temper the overall impact somewhat.

Score: 7

The score of 7 reflects the solid contribution and potential of TaxoAdapt, while acknowledging the limitations and areas for future exploration. It is a significant advance over existing methods, warranting further investigation and wider application.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Integrating Large Language Models into Text Animation: An Intelligent Editing System with Inline and Chat Interaction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10762v1)
- **Authors**: Bao Zhang, Zihan Li, Zhenglei Liu, Huanchen Wang, Yuxin Ma
- **Abstract**: Text animation, a foundational element in video creation, enables efficient and cost-effective communication, thriving in advertisements, journalism, and social media. However, traditional animation workflows present significant usability barriers for non-professionals, with intricate operational procedures severely hindering creative productivity. To address this, we propose a Large Language Model (LLM)-aided text animation editing system that enables real-time intent tracking and flexible editing. The system introduces an agent-based dual-stream pipeline that integrates context-aware inline suggestions and conversational guidance as well as employs a semantic-animation mapping to facilitate LLM-driven creative intent translation. Besides, the system supports synchronized text-animation previews and parametric adjustments via unified controls to improve editing workflow. A user study evaluates the system, highlighting its ability to help non-professional users complete animation workflows while validating the pipeline. The findings encourage further exploration of integrating LLMs into a comprehensive video creation workflow.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the provided research paper titled "Integrating Large Language Models into Text Animation: An Intelligent Editing System with Inline and Chat Interaction". Here's a summary, followed by a critical evaluation of its novelty and significance, and finally a score with justification.

**Summary**

The paper introduces a novel text animation editing system that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to streamline the creation and refinement process. Addressing the usability barriers faced by non-professionals in traditional animation workflows, the system offers a dual-stream agent-based pipeline: an Inline Agent for context-aware suggestions and a Chat Agent for conversational guidance.  The system uses a semantic-animation mapping for creative intent translation, and a synchronized script-timeline interface, simplifying the editing workflow. A user study with 11 participants demonstrates the system's ability to aid non-professionals in creating animations effectively. The paper emphasizes the importance of human-AI collaboration and offers insights into integrating LLMs for multimedia content creation. The contributions include a formative study, an interactive system with LLM-powered agents, semantic-animation mapping, a dual-mode pipeline, and a user study.

**Critical Evaluation**

*Novelty:*

The paper demonstrates novelty in a few key areas, particularly the combination of several existing ideas.  Specifically:

*   **Dual-Stream Agent Approach:** The integration of both an inline suggestion agent and a conversational chat agent is a relatively novel approach. While using agents for video editing is not completely new, the specific combination and the way they interact with the user provide a more flexible and intuitive workflow.
*   **Semantic-Animation Mapping:** While the idea of using natural language to control video elements exists, the paper explicitly describes a structured mapping between semantic meaning and animation parameters. However, the specifics of this mapping (which features are mapped and how) are not sufficiently detailed, and this could be seen as an incremental improvement.
*   **Script-Timeline Synchronization:** The real-time synchronization between script and timeline panels aims to bridge the gap between scriptwriting and video editing.  Similar features exist in professional tools, but the explicit focus on seamless integration and the LLM-assisted suggestions within this context adds to the novelty.

The degree of novelty is somewhat limited by the fact that many components have been individually explored in previous research.  The integration is the key, but the innovation is not groundbreaking.

*Significance:*

The paper's significance lies primarily in its potential to lower the barrier to entry for text animation creation. By simplifying complex workflows and providing intelligent assistance, the system could empower a broader range of users to create engaging video content.  The user study, although limited in scale, provides evidence of this potential.

*   **Improved Usability:** The system offers a more intuitive and user-friendly interface compared to traditional video editing software, which can be difficult for non-professionals to navigate.
*   **Enhanced Creativity:**  The LLM-powered agents provide creative suggestions and assistance, potentially inspiring users to explore new ideas and approaches.
*   **Practical Application:** The system has clear applications in various domains, including social media marketing, education, and content creation for small businesses.

However, the significance is somewhat constrained by the current limitations of LLMs (e.g., response time, potential for hallucination). These limitations, if not adequately addressed, could hinder the overall effectiveness of the system. Moreover, the user study has a relatively small sample size and is conducted in a controlled setting, which may not accurately reflect real-world usage scenarios. Furthermore, it lacks a detailed comparison against baseline methods for animation creation, limiting the ability to quantify the improvements offered by the LLM based assistance.

*Strengths:*

*   Clear problem statement and motivation.
*   Well-defined design goals and considerations.
*   Comprehensive system architecture.
*   User study providing initial evidence of effectiveness.

*Weaknesses:*

*   Details on Semantic-Animation Mapping are lacking.
*   Relatively small sample size for the user study, and lack of baseline methods in the evaluation.
*   Over-reliance on current LLM capabilities without fully addressing their limitations.
*   The description lacks a detailed quantitative analysis of the performance gains (e.g. creation time reduction or accuracy increase due to LLM integration).

**Justification for the Score:**

Considering the above analysis, the paper presents a *moderately* novel approach with *significant* potential in lowering the barrier to entry in text animation. The combination of LLM-powered agents, semantic mapping, and synchronized interfaces is promising, but the individual components are not entirely new, and the reliance on LLM capabilities necessitates careful consideration. The limited user study further constrains the current evidence of impact.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 6/10

### OPT-BENCH: Evaluating LLM Agent on Large-Scale Search Spaces Optimization Problems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10764v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaozhe Li, Jixuan Chen, Xinyu Fang, Shengyuan Ding, Haodong Duan, Qingwen Liu, Kai Chen
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown remarkable capabilities in solving diverse tasks. However, their proficiency in iteratively optimizing complex solutions through learning from previous feedback remains insufficiently explored. To bridge this gap, we present OPT-BENCH, a comprehensive benchmark designed to evaluate LLM agents on large-scale search space optimization problems. OPT-BENCH includes 20 real-world machine learning tasks sourced from Kaggle and 10 classical NP problems, offering a diverse and challenging environment for assessing LLM agents on iterative reasoning and solution refinement. To enable rigorous evaluation, we introduce OPT-Agent, an end-to-end optimization framework that emulates human reasoning when tackling complex problems by generating, validating, and iteratively improving solutions through leveraging historical feedback. Through extensive experiments on 9 state-of-the-art LLMs from 6 model families, we analyze the effects of optimization iterations, temperature settings, and model architectures on solution quality and convergence. Our results demonstrate that incorporating historical context significantly enhances optimization performance across both ML and NP tasks. All datasets, code, and evaluation tools are open-sourced to promote further research in advancing LLM-driven optimization and iterative reasoning. Project page: \href{https://github.com/OliverLeeXZ/OPT-BENCH}{https://github.com/OliverLeeXZ/OPT-BENCH}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces OPT-BENCH, a benchmark designed to evaluate the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to solve optimization problems within large search spaces through iterative reasoning and learning from feedback. OPT-BENCH includes 20 real-world machine learning tasks from Kaggle and 10 classical NP-complete problems. To facilitate rigorous evaluation, the authors also present OPT-Agent, an end-to-end optimization framework that simulates human problem-solving by generating, validating, and iteratively improving solutions based on historical feedback.  The paper evaluates the performance of several LLMs using OPT-BENCH and OPT-Agent, analyzes the impact of optimization iterations, temperature settings, and model architectures, and demonstrates that incorporating historical context enhances optimization performance. The authors open-source their datasets, code, and evaluation tools.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its creation of a comprehensive benchmark (OPT-BENCH) specifically designed to evaluate LLMs' *iterative optimization capabilities* on both ML and NP problems. While existing benchmarks focus on single-pass solution generation or reasoning, OPT-BENCH emphasizes learning from experience and adapting over multiple iterations. The OPT-Agent framework, while inspired by existing agent-based approaches, is tailored to the specific needs of iterative optimization, providing a valuable tool for evaluating LLMs in this context. The combination of real-world Kaggle tasks and NP problems provides a more holistic evaluation compared to benchmarks focused solely on one domain.
*   **Significance:** The ability of LLMs to perform iterative optimization and learn from feedback is crucial for tackling complex, real-world problems. OPT-BENCH addresses a gap in existing LLM evaluations by focusing on this crucial aspect. The benchmark could spur further research in enhancing LLMs' optimization skills, potentially leading to more effective solutions in various domains, including machine learning, combinatorial optimization, and automated algorithm design. The analysis of different LLM architectures and hyperparameters provides valuable insights for practitioners.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Benchmark:** The inclusion of both ML and NP problems provides a well-rounded evaluation.
    *   **Realistic Tasks:** Kaggle competitions bring a degree of real-world complexity that synthetic benchmarks often lack.
    *   **End-to-End Framework:** OPT-Agent automates the evaluation process and allows for rigorous experimentation.
    *   **Open-Sourced Resources:** Promoting reproducibility and encouraging further research.
    *   **Thorough Analysis:** The paper presents a detailed analysis of the experimental results, highlighting the impact of various factors on LLM performance.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Cost:** The iterative nature of the evaluation can be computationally expensive, potentially limiting the scale of experiments or the number of models that can be evaluated. The paper touches upon potential methods for window reduction, but further exploration of scalability could improve future research efforts.
    *   **Potential for Bias:** Kaggle competitions, while realistic, can be influenced by the design choices and biases of the original competition organizers. It's possible that the tasks in OPT-BENCH favor certain types of models or optimization strategies.
    *   **NP Problem Limitations:** The paper admits that leveraging historical information is less effective for NP problems compared to ML ones, which indicates some limitations with the OPT-Agent approach. In these cases, historical data may have limited usefulness.
    *   **Evaluation Metric Simplifications**: Given the diversity of ML tasks, IR may be overly simplistic and does not explicitly account for data scale variation and other heterogeneities.
*   **Impact:** OPT-BENCH has the potential to become a standard benchmark for evaluating LLMs in iterative optimization tasks. The OPT-Agent framework could also be adopted by other researchers as a tool for developing and evaluating new LLM-based optimization techniques. The insights from the paper's experiments can guide the design of more effective LLMs for complex problem-solving.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **7**. While OPT-BENCH fills a crucial gap in LLM evaluation and has the potential for significant impact, there are limitations regarding the computational cost, potential for bias in task selection, and simplification of the diversity between Kaggle tasks. Further research to address these weaknesses would strengthen the benchmark and increase its overall value. The paper presents a worthwhile contribution and is likely to guide and focus future work on the subject. The open-sourcing of materials further increases this probability.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### One Tokenizer To Rule Them All: Emergent Language Plasticity via Multilingual Tokenizers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10766v1)
- **Authors**: Diana Abagyan, Alejandro R. Salamanca, Andres Felipe Cruz-Salinas, Kris Cao, Hangyu Lin, Acyr Locatelli, Marzieh Fadaee, Ahmet Üstün, Sara Hooker
- **Abstract**: Pretraining massively multilingual Large Language Models (LLMs) for many languages at once is challenging due to limited model capacity, scarce high-quality data, and compute constraints. Moreover, the lack of language coverage of the tokenizer makes it harder to address the gap for new languages purely at the post-training stage. In this work, we study what relatively cheap interventions early on in training improve "language plasticity", or adaptation capabilities of the model post-training to new languages. We focus on tokenizer design and propose using a universal tokenizer that is trained for more languages than the primary pretraining languages to enable efficient adaptation in expanding language coverage after pretraining. Our systematic experiments across diverse groups of languages and different training strategies show that a universal tokenizer enables significantly higher language adaptation, with up to 20.2% increase in win rates compared to tokenizers specific to pretraining languages. Furthermore, a universal tokenizer also leads to better plasticity towards languages that are completely unseen in the tokenizer and pretraining, by up to 5% win rate gain. We achieve this adaptation to an expanded set of languages with minimal compromise in performance on the majority of languages included in pretraining.
- **Summary**: The paper "One Tokenizer To Rule Them All: Emergent Language Plasticity via Multilingual Tokenizers" investigates how to improve the language adaptation capabilities (plasticity) of multilingual Large Language Models (LLMs) during the computationally expensive pre-training phase. The core idea is to use a "universal tokenizer" trained on a broader set of languages than the primary pre-training languages. The authors hypothesize that this approach enables more efficient adaptation to new languages after pre-training.  Through systematic experiments across diverse language groups and training strategies, they demonstrate that a universal tokenizer leads to significantly higher language adaptation, showing improvements in win rates compared to tokenizers specific to pre-training languages. They also demonstrate improved plasticity towards languages completely unseen in the tokenizer and pre-training. The adaptation is achieved with minimal compromise on performance in the primarily pre-trained languages. The paper finds that the UNIVERSAL tokenizer enables faster adaptation performance, requiring less additional training. They also explore the impact of vocabulary size and the presence of expanded language subset data in pretraining. The authors compare their method to cross-lingual vocabulary adaptation (CVA) techniques.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

The core novelty of this paper lies in its focus on the tokenizer design as a key lever to enhance the *plasticity* of multilingual LLMs during the *pre-training* stage.  Prior work has explored vocabulary expansion or embedding layer retraining *after* pre-training. This paper's proactive approach to tokenizer design as an early intervention for improved adaptation is the primary contribution. The extensive ablations performed, varying tokenizer design, language subsets, and adaptation strategies, are a strength and demonstrate a comprehensive examination of their hypothesis.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Focus:** The emphasis on tokenizer design for improving *pretraining plasticity* is a valuable and relatively unexplored area.
*   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experimental setup is thorough and well-designed, with ablations addressing different aspects of tokenizer design and adaptation strategies. The scale of the experiments (69 languages) is commendable.
*   **Empirical Evidence:** The paper provides strong empirical evidence supporting the effectiveness of the universal tokenizer, demonstrating improvements in adaptation performance across diverse language groups.
*   **Practical Implications:** The findings have practical implications for researchers and practitioners who want to expand the language coverage of pre-trained models without significant additional costs. The reduced adaptation time and data requirements are particularly attractive.
*   **Comparison to Baselines**:  The comparison against a more specialized cluster-specific tokenizer provides a strong baseline to contextualize the benefits of the proposed approach.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited architectural variation**:  The models and architecture considered could be seen as a limitation (one model size, one architecture).  The paper would be strengthened by experiments on a more diverse set of architectures to support generalizability.
*   **Judge Model Dependency:**  The evaluation depends heavily on LLM-as-a-judge win rates. The paper addresses this by selecting Command-A, a strong open-weights judge, and referencing literature supporting its correlation with human judgments, but this reliance introduces a potential source of bias. Additional task-specific evaluations offer partial mitigation, but more explicit investigation of the judge's influence could be valuable.
*   **Modest performance gain over CVA**:  While UNIVERSAL tokenizers outperform CVA methods, the gains aren't *massive* and could benefit from further investigation regarding edge cases or when each may be more preferable. The gains on the fully unseen languages are good.
*   **Limited vocabulary sizes explored**: The range of vocabulary sizes explored in the ablation could be expanded in future work.

**Significance:**

The paper's findings have significant implications for the efficient development and deployment of multilingual LLMs. By demonstrating the effectiveness of a universal tokenizer in improving language plasticity, the authors offer a valuable tool for addressing the language coverage gap in existing models.  The reduced adaptation costs and data requirements could empower researchers and practitioners to extend the reach of LLMs to a wider range of languages, including those that are under-resourced. The paper could lead to a shift in how tokenizers are designed for multilingual models, with greater emphasis on promoting pretraining plasticity.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel approach to improving language adaptation in multilingual LLMs through careful tokenizer design.  The rigorous experimental evaluation and practical implications of the findings warrant a high score. However, the reliance on LLM-as-a-judge evaluations, the limited architecture exploration, and the modest gains compared to some CVA techniques suggest that there is room for further improvement and refinement.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Different Questions, Different Models: Fine-Grained Evaluation of Uncertainty and Calibration in Clinical QA with LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10769v1)
- **Authors**: Alberto Testoni, Iacer Calixto
- **Abstract**: Accurate and well-calibrated uncertainty estimates are essential for deploying large language models (LLMs) in high-stakes domains such as clinical decision support. We present a fine-grained evaluation of uncertainty estimation methods for clinical multiple-choice question answering, covering ten open-source LLMs (general-purpose, biomedical, and reasoning models) across two datasets, eleven medical specialties, and six question types. We compare standard single-generation and sampling-based methods, and present a case study exploring simple, single-pass estimators based on behavioral signals in reasoning traces. These lightweight methods approach the performance of Semantic Entropy while requiring only one generation. Our results reveal substantial variation across specialties and question types, underscoring the importance of selecting models based on both the nature of the question and model-specific strengths.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a comprehensive evaluation of uncertainty quantification (UQ) methods for Large Language Models (LLMs) applied to clinical question answering.  The study covers ten open-source LLMs (general, biomedical, and reasoning models), two datasets (S-MedQA and MedExQA), eleven medical specialties (common and less represented), and six question types (diagnosis, treatment, etc.).  The authors compare standard single-generation and sampling-based uncertainty estimators.  They also introduce and evaluate a novel single-pass estimation method based on behavioral signals extracted from LLM reasoning traces (response length, self-questioning, and self-verification).  The results highlight substantial variations in UQ performance across specialties and question types, underscoring the importance of model selection tailored to both the question and model strengths. The paper also finds that reasoning models have an advantage in accuracy and uncertainty quantification over other models. It also shows that the method of semantic entropy tends to have better estimations. Finally, behavioral signals from reasoning traces can serve as proxies for uncertainty.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Scope:**  The paper's strength lies in its extensive evaluation, spanning a large set of models, datasets, specialties, and question types. This provides a much more nuanced understanding of UQ performance in clinical NLP than previous studies, which tended to be limited in scope.
    *   **Fine-Grained Analysis:** The analysis by medical specialty and question type is a significant contribution. It reveals that UQ performance is not uniform and that general performance metrics can mask critical failures in specific, high-risk areas.
    *   **Novel Method:**  The introduction of a single-pass UQ method based on behavioral signals is a valuable addition to the field. This method offers a potentially more efficient alternative to sampling-based methods, making it more practical for real-world deployment. It has significant potential value since it can perform well when sampling cannot be done due to constraints.
    *   **Emphasis on Calibration:** The paper rightly emphasizes the importance of calibration in clinical applications and provides a thorough assessment using appropriate metrics (ECE, Brier Score).
    *   **Practical Guidance:** The paper offers practical recommendations for model selection based on the specific clinical context (specialty and question type), which is highly valuable for practitioners.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Behavioral Signals:** While the behavioral signal approach is promising, the features used (number of tokens, self-posed questions, "Wait" tokens) are relatively simple and task-specific. The degree to which these findings generalize to other tasks, languages, or reasoning paradigms is unclear.
    *   **Dataset Characteristics:** The use of multiple-choice question answering might limit the generalizability of the findings to more open-ended clinical NLP tasks.
    *   **Reliance on Open-Source Models:** Restricting the model set to open-source models limits the applicability of the findings, although the model set remains relatively large and expansive.
    *   **Heuristics/Automated Processes:** Reliance on automated processes to perform tasks (question type annotation, answer extraction, etc.) can lead to residual noise.
    *   **Temperature Scaling and Post Hoc Calibration:** The paper doesn't make use of the more complex calibration techniques that exist, which could enhance performance.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The paper demonstrably expands on prior work by conducting a more comprehensive and fine-grained evaluation of UQ methods in clinical NLP. The novel single-pass behavioral signal method is a valuable contribution. The practical guidelines for model selection are useful for the community.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    The paper has the potential to influence the field by:

    *   Encouraging more rigorous and fine-grained evaluation of LLMs in clinical applications.
    *   Promoting the development of more efficient and interpretable UQ methods.
    *   Guiding practitioners in selecting appropriate models for specific clinical tasks.

*   **Justification for Score:**

    The paper makes a significant contribution by providing a detailed and nuanced evaluation of UQ in clinical QA, especially through its analyses of different specialties and question types. The introduction of the behavioral signal approach offers a novel way to enhance UQ efficiency. The extensive experimentation and analysis justify a high score. However, the reliance on multiple-choice data and simple features slightly reduces the impact of the paper.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### ME: Trigger Element Combination Backdoor Attack on Copyright Infringement
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10776v1)
- **Authors**: Feiyu Yang, Siyuan Liang, Aishan Liu, Dacheng Tao
- **Abstract**: The capability of generative diffusion models (DMs) like Stable Diffusion (SD) in replicating training data could be taken advantage of by attackers to launch the Copyright Infringement Attack, with duplicated poisoned image-text pairs. SilentBadDiffusion (SBD) is a method proposed recently, which shew outstanding performance in attacking SD in text-to-image tasks. However, the feasible data resources in this area are still limited, some of them are even constrained or prohibited due to the issues like copyright ownership or inappropriate contents; And not all of the images in current datasets are suitable for the proposed attacking methods; Besides, the state-of-the-art (SoTA) performance of SBD is far from ideal when few generated poisoning samples could be adopted for attacks. In this paper, we raised new datasets accessible for researching in attacks like SBD, and proposed Multi-Element (ME) attack method based on SBD by increasing the number of poisonous visual-text elements per poisoned sample to enhance the ability of attacking, while importing Discrete Cosine Transform (DCT) for the poisoned samples to maintain the stealthiness. The Copyright Infringement Rate (CIR) / First Attack Epoch (FAE) we got on the two new datasets were 16.78% / 39.50 and 51.20% / 23.60, respectively close to or even outperformed benchmark Pokemon and Mijourney datasets. In condition of low subsampling ratio (5%, 6 poisoned samples), MESI and DCT earned CIR / FAE of 0.23% / 84.00 and 12.73% / 65.50, both better than original SBD, which failed to attack at all.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of copyright infringement attacks on text-to-image diffusion models like Stable Diffusion (SD). It focuses on overcoming limitations of the existing SilentBadDiffusion (SBD) attack, which suffers from limited data resources and performance degradation when only a small fraction of poisoned samples are used in training.  The authors propose two main contributions: (1) introducing two new datasets (Style and DiffusionDB) for researching these attacks and (2) developing a modified attack method called Multi-Element (ME) that increases the number of trigger elements per poisoned sample and utilizes Discrete Cosine Transform (DCT) to maintain stealthiness. Experimental results on the new datasets demonstrate improved Copyright Infringement Rate (CIR) and First Attack Epoch (FAE) compared to the original SBD attack, especially under low subsampling ratios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**
    *   **Datasets:** The introduction of Style and DiffusionDB is a notable contribution. The scarcity of accessible and legally sound datasets in this domain is a genuine challenge.  These datasets could serve as useful benchmarks for future research.
    *   **Method:** The ME attack with DCT is a relatively incremental improvement over SBD. The idea of increasing trigger elements per sample is intuitive, and the use of DCT for stealthiness is a standard image processing technique.  While the combination is logical, the novelty is moderate.

*   **Significance:**
    *   **Practical Relevance:** Copyright infringement is a growing concern in the age of generative AI. This paper directly addresses a relevant and important problem. Demonstrating that these models are susceptible to subtle poisoning attacks has practical implications for both developers and users.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The paper provides a thorough experimental evaluation of the proposed datasets and methods.  The comparisons to the existing SBD attack are well-designed, and the results demonstrate clear performance improvements, especially in challenging scenarios with low poisoning ratios.
    *   **Lack of Robustness Analysis:** A major limitation of the paper is lack of robustness analysis against existing defenses like ABL, especially since the authors also explored it, in the appendix.

*   **Limitations:**
    *   **Incremental Contribution:** While the proposed methods improve upon SBD, they build directly upon existing techniques. The core idea of using diffusion models for copyright infringement attacks was already established. The DCT method has already been used to conceal data and preserve privacy of images.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The experiments focus primarily on specific diffusion models and datasets. Generalizability to other architectures and tasks is not thoroughly explored.
    *   **Stealthiness Validation:** While the paper claims that the DCT helps maintain stealthiness, it primarily focuses on perceptual similarity metrics. A more rigorous analysis of the statistical properties of the poisoned samples and their detectability would strengthen this claim.
    *   **Lack of Thoroughness:** The datasets lack rigorous quality checks.

*   **Overall:** The paper makes a solid contribution by addressing a practical problem and providing useful datasets. The proposed attack method, while somewhat incremental, achieves demonstrable improvements over the existing state-of-the-art, particularly in realistic scenarios with limited poisoning data. However, the incremental nature, coupled with lack of robustness validation against existing defenses and limited scope, brings the impact down.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a valuable contribution to a relevant field, copyright infringement attacks in diffusion models, which should not be ignored in light of recent copyright infringement law suites. It introduces new datasets and methods that demonstrate improved attack effectiveness in certain scenarios. However, the limited scope, reliance on standard image processing techniques, and incremental nature of the improvements, as well as lack of robustness evaluation against countermeasures, prevent it from achieving a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 6/10

### What Users Value and Critique: Large-Scale Analysis of User Feedback on AI-Powered Mobile Apps
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10785v1)
- **Authors**: Vinaik Chhetri, Krishna Upadhyay, A. B. Siddique, Umar Farooq
- **Abstract**: Artificial Intelligence (AI)-powered features have rapidly proliferated across mobile apps in various domains, including productivity, education, entertainment, and creativity. However, how users perceive, evaluate, and critique these AI features remains largely unexplored, primarily due to the overwhelming volume of user feedback. In this work, we present the first comprehensive, large-scale study of user feedback on AI-powered mobile apps, leveraging a curated dataset of 292 AI-driven apps across 14 categories with 894K AI-specific reviews from Google Play. We develop and validate a multi-stage analysis pipeline that begins with a human-labeled benchmark and systematically evaluates large language models (LLMs) and prompting strategies. Each stage, including review classification, aspect-sentiment extraction, and clustering, is validated for accuracy and consistency. Our pipeline enables scalable, high-precision analysis of user feedback, extracting over one million aspect-sentiment pairs clustered into 18 positive and 15 negative user topics. Our analysis reveals that users consistently focus on a narrow set of themes: positive comments emphasize productivity, reliability, and personalized assistance, while negative feedback highlights technical failures (e.g., scanning and recognition), pricing concerns, and limitations in language support. Our pipeline surfaces both satisfaction with one feature and frustration with another within the same review. These fine-grained, co-occurring sentiments are often missed by traditional approaches that treat positive and negative feedback in isolation or rely on coarse-grained analysis. To this end, our approach provides a more faithful reflection of the real-world user experiences with AI-powered apps. Category-aware analysis further uncovers both universal drivers of satisfaction and domain-specific frustrations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a large-scale analysis of user feedback on AI-powered mobile apps. The authors curated a dataset of 292 AI-driven apps from Google Play across 14 categories, extracting over 890K AI-specific reviews. They developed and validated a multi-stage LLM-based pipeline for review classification, aspect-sentiment extraction, and topic clustering. This pipeline enabled them to identify key themes in user feedback, highlighting valued AI capabilities (productivity, reliability, personalization) and shortfalls (technical failures, pricing, language limitations). The analysis further explores the co-occurrence of positive and negative feedback within the same review and examines how user perceptions vary across different app categories. The paper concludes with actionable recommendations for developers on how to align AI features with user expectations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper fills a significant gap in the literature by providing the first comprehensive, large-scale analysis of user feedback on AI-powered mobile apps. Prior work has primarily focused on general mobile app reviews or narrow AI-specific applications. The category-aware analysis is a particularly strong point, offering insights beyond aggregated trends.
*   **Methodological Rigor:** The authors employ a well-defined and validated multi-stage pipeline using state-of-the-art LLMs. The benchmark dataset construction, inter-rater agreement checks, and performance comparisons of various prompting strategies contribute to the rigor of the study.  The focus on fine-grained aspect-sentiment extraction, rather than coarse-grained review analysis, enables a more nuanced understanding of user experiences. The clustering approach, using semantic embeddings, is also well-justified.
*   **Actionable Insights:** The paper provides clear and actionable recommendations for software engineers and app developers. These recommendations are grounded in empirical evidence and offer valuable guidance for aligning AI features with user expectations.  The identification of dual-role topics (those eliciting both positive and negative feedback) is particularly insightful, highlighting areas requiring careful attention.
*   **Reproducibility:** The authors make their dataset, LLM-based pipeline code, and clustering artifacts publicly available, enhancing the reproducibility and impact of their work.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Google Play Descriptions:** The definition of "AI-driven apps" relies on descriptions in the Google Play Store. While practical, this may introduce biases or inaccuracies, as app developers might selectively advertise AI features. Some apps may legitimately leverage AI without explicitly stating it, and vice versa.
*   **English Language Bias:** The study focuses exclusively on English-language reviews, limiting its generalizability to diverse user populations and app categories. The global app market necessitates accounting for different linguistic and cultural contexts.
*   **Static View of User Expectations:** The analysis captures a snapshot of user perceptions at a specific point in time. User expectations of AI are rapidly evolving, and longitudinal studies are needed to track these changes.
*   **Potential for LLM Bias:** Although the LLMs were benchmarked, the potential for bias in the LLM's classification and extraction should be acknowledged.  While human validation was performed, it's possible that subtle biases could remain.
*  ** Limited discussion on ethics** The paper does not adequately address the ethical implications of AI. While the code is available, it should contain a section on ethical considerations of such large data analysis.

**Significance:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the understanding of user perceptions of AI in mobile apps. It moves beyond anecdotal evidence and provides a systematic, data-driven analysis that can inform the design and development of AI-powered features. The findings are relevant to software engineers, product managers, and user experience researchers working in the field of AI and mobile applications. The identified challenges and opportunities can help shape the future of AI integration in mobile apps, leading to more user-centered and effective solutions.

**Score:** 8

**Justification:**

The paper's novelty, methodological rigor, actionable insights, and reproducibility justify a high score. It addresses a relevant and under-explored area with a well-executed study. While the limitations related to the definition of AI-driven apps, language bias, static view, LLM bias, and limited ethical considerations prevent it from receiving a higher score, the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses. It provides a valuable foundation for future research in this rapidly evolving domain.
- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 8/10

### FASCIST-O-METER: Classifier for Neo-fascist Discourse Online
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10789v1)
- **Authors**: Rudy Alexandro Garrido Veliz, Martin Semmann, Chris Biemann, Seid Muhie Yimam
- **Abstract**: Neo-fascism is a political and societal ideology that has been having remarkable growth in the last decade in the United States of America (USA), as well as in other Western societies. It poses a grave danger to democracy and the minorities it targets, and it requires active actions against it to avoid escalation. This work presents the first-of-its-kind neo-fascist coding scheme for digital discourse in the USA societal context, overseen by political science researchers. Our work bridges the gap between Natural Language Processing (NLP) and political science against this phenomena. Furthermore, to test the coding scheme, we collect a tremendous amount of activity on the internet from notable neo-fascist groups (the forums of Iron March and Stormfront.org), and the guidelines are applied to a subset of the collected posts. Through crowdsourcing, we annotate a total of a thousand posts that are labeled as neo-fascist or non-neo-fascist. With this labeled data set, we fine-tune and test both Small Language Models (SLMs) and Large Language Models (LLMs), obtaining the very first classification models for neo-fascist discourse. We find that the prevalence of neo-fascist rhetoric in this kind of forum is ever-present, making them a good target for future research. The societal context is a key consideration for neo-fascist speech when conducting NLP research. Finally, the work against this kind of political movement must be pressed upon and continued for the well-being of a democratic society. Disclaimer: This study focuses on detecting neo-fascist content in text, similar to other hate speech analyses, without labeling individuals or organizations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces "FASCIST-O-METER," a system for classifying neo-fascist discourse online, specifically within the U.S. societal context. The work addresses the growing prevalence and dangers of neo-fascism by bridging the gap between Natural Language Processing (NLP) and political science research.  The authors create a novel neo-fascist coding scheme based on political science theory, collect a large dataset from known neo-fascist forums (Iron March and Stormfront.org), and annotate a subset of the data through crowdsourcing. They then fine-tune both small and large language models (SLMs and LLMs) to classify neo-fascist content. The study investigates the use of NLP tools for identifying neo-fascist discourse, tackles the challenges in annotating such content, and evaluates the performance of various language models. The paper concludes that neo-fascist rhetoric is prevalent in these forums and emphasizes the importance of societal context when analyzing neo-fascist speech.

**Critical Evaluation**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its attempt to create the first dedicated NLP classification system for neo-fascist discourse. While there's existing work on hate speech and extremism detection, the focus on the specific ideological framework of neo-fascism, particularly within the U.S. context, adds a layer of specialization. The combination of political science theory with NLP techniques is another valuable aspect. The creation of the novel coding scheme is good. 
* **Significance:** The significance of this research is high. Neo-fascism is a dangerous ideology, and automated detection could be a valuable tool for researchers, policymakers, and potentially content moderators. Identifying and understanding online neo-fascist discourse is crucial for developing effective counter-strategies and mitigating its real-world impact. The authors directly address a societal problem.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Multi-Disciplinary Approach:** Integrates political science and NLP, which is crucial for addressing complex, context-dependent issues like neo-fascist discourse.
    * **Carefully Developed Coding Scheme:** The coding scheme is grounded in political science research.
    * **Dataset Collection:** The large-scale collection of data from relevant online forums provides a valuable resource for future research.
    * **Model Evaluation:** The paper evaluates several SLMs and LLMs, giving a decent overview of the state of the art in this classification task.
    * **Important topic:** Tackling neo-fascism is of significant societal and political relevance.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Annotation Size:** The annotation is 1000 posts (3000 entries) from a dataset of ~650,000 posts.
    * **MTurk Quality:** While some quality control was applied during crowdsourcing, MTurk datasets are known to have significant noise. More thorough manual review, and a more clearly described inter-annotator agreement process, would improve the dataset.
    * **Limited Error Analysis:** Error analysis is light and high-level, missing an opportunity to analyze what the best model got wrong, and if those errors can provide more insight for better labels.
    * **Lack of Contemporary Data** The Stormfront dataset is from 2020 or earlier and Iron March is even older. Neo-fascist discourse may have shifted strategies and evolved language since then, limiting the real-world applicability of models trained on this data.

* **Potential Impact:** The FASCIST-O-METER has the potential to inform the development of more sophisticated detection systems, aid in understanding neo-fascist narratives, and contribute to broader efforts to combat online extremism.
* **Room for Improvement:**
    * **Expand the Annotated Dataset:** A larger, meticulously annotated dataset would significantly improve the robustness and generalizability of the models.
    * **Incorporate More Contextual Features:**  The models could benefit from incorporating additional contextual features, such as user metadata, network relationships, and temporal information.
    * **Address Code-Switching and Evolving Language:**  Neo-fascist discourse often employs code-switching and adapts to evade detection. The models need to be robust to these strategies.
    * **Address Societal Shift** Update the dataset to take into account societal shifts and ensure it remains up to date.

**Overall, the paper is a valuable first step toward developing automated tools for detecting neo-fascist discourse. It has decent novelty and importance, but there are significant shortcomings in the annotation process and the currency of the underlying data. These limit the potential impact of the work. As such, I would rate this work with a 5.**

Score: 5

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 5/10

### Mitigating Negative Interference in Multilingual Sequential Knowledge Editing through Null-Space Constraints
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10800v1)
- **Authors**: Wei Sun, Tingyu Qu, Mingxiao Li, Jesse Davis, Marie-Francine Moens
- **Abstract**: Efficiently updating multilingual knowledge in large language models (LLMs), while preserving consistent factual representations across languages, remains a long-standing and unresolved challenge. While deploying separate editing systems for each language might seem viable, this approach incurs substantial costs due to the need to manage multiple models. A more efficient solution involves integrating knowledge updates across all languages into a unified model. However, performing sequential edits across languages often leads to destructive parameter interference, significantly degrading multilingual generalization and the accuracy of injected knowledge. To address this challenge, we propose LangEdit, a novel null-space constrained framework designed to precisely isolate language-specific knowledge updates. The core innovation of LangEdit lies in its ability to project parameter updates for each language onto the orthogonal complement of previous updated subspaces. This approach mathematically guarantees update independence while preserving multilingual generalization capabilities. We conduct a comprehensive evaluation across three model architectures, six languages, and four downstream tasks, demonstrating that LangEdit effectively mitigates parameter interference and outperforms existing state-of-the-art editing methods. Our results highlight its potential for enabling efficient and accurate multilingual knowledge updates in LLMs. The code is available at https://github.com/VRCMF/LangEdit.git.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of negative interference in multilingual sequential knowledge editing of Large Language Models (LLMs).  The authors introduce LangEdit, a novel framework that uses null-space constraints to isolate language-specific knowledge updates.  By projecting parameter updates for each language onto the orthogonal complement of previously updated subspaces, LangEdit aims to guarantee update independence and preserve multilingual generalization capabilities. The paper presents a comprehensive evaluation across multiple models, languages, and downstream tasks, showing that LangEdit effectively mitigates parameter interference and outperforms existing editing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the application of null-space constraints specifically to the *multilingual sequential* knowledge editing problem.  While null-space projection has been used in continual learning and related areas, its adaptation to this specific task is a significant contribution. Existing knowledge editing methods are primarily designed for monolingual scenarios, neglecting the critical issue of cross-lingual interference. The idea of "language safeguards" by preventing parameter conflicts is quite insightful.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the increasing importance of multilingual LLMs and the need for efficient and accurate knowledge editing in these models. Current solutions that deploy separate editing systems per language are unsustainable due to high resource costs. LangEdit offers a more efficient alternative by integrating knowledge updates into a single model while mitigating interference. The demonstrated improvements in both editing accuracy and multilingual generalization are compelling.  Knowledge-informed multilingual information retrieval is a growing area, making this work highly relevant. The paper establishes a new benchmark for multilingual sequential knowledge editing with the construction of several multilingual datasets.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly defines the problem of negative interference in multilingual sequential knowledge editing, which is often overlooked in monolingual editing research.
    *   **Technical Approach:**  The null-space projection method is mathematically sound and provides a principled way to isolate language-specific updates.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:**  The comprehensive evaluation across multiple models, languages, and downstream tasks is a major strength. The results convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of LangEdit.
    *   **Reproducibility:**  The paper provides code and implementation details to enable reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost:** Although the paper assesses computational cost, the increased complexity of LangEdit compared to simpler methods (like fine-tuning) might be a barrier to adoption. A more detailed analysis, including wall-clock time and scalability to larger models, would be valuable.
    *   **Language Scope:** While the experiments include several languages, the linguistic diversity is somewhat limited. Extending the evaluation to more typologically diverse languages would strengthen the findings.
    *   **Reliance on Feature Covariance:** The method depends on estimating feature covariance matrices. This estimation process may introduce its own approximations or biases, and the sensitivity of LangEdit to the accuracy of these estimates is not fully explored.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to have a significant impact on the field of knowledge editing for LLMs. It introduces a novel and effective approach to a critical problem, sets a new benchmark for multilingual sequential knowledge editing, and provides a strong foundation for future research in this area. Other researchers can now build upon this work to develop even more robust and efficient multilingual editing techniques.

**Score: 8.5**

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of knowledge editing for LLMs, specifically addressing the crucial issue of negative interference in multilingual settings. The proposed method, LangEdit, is technically sound, and the comprehensive evaluation provides strong empirical evidence of its effectiveness. While there are some weaknesses related to computational cost, language scope, and the dependence on feature covariance estimation, the overall impact of the paper is substantial. The establishment of a new benchmark and the public release of code will further accelerate research in this area. The score reflects the paper's high degree of novelty, significance, and technical rigor.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Detecting High-Stakes Interactions with Activation Probes
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10805v1)
- **Authors**: Alex McKenzie, Urja Pawar, Phil Blandfort, William Bankes, David Krueger, Ekdeep Singh Lubana, Dmitrii Krasheninnikov
- **Abstract**: Monitoring is an important aspect of safely deploying Large Language Models (LLMs). This paper examines activation probes for detecting "high-stakes" interactions -- where the text indicates that the interaction might lead to significant harm -- as a critical, yet underexplored, target for such monitoring. We evaluate several probe architectures trained on synthetic data, and find them to exhibit robust generalization to diverse, out-of-distribution, real-world data. Probes' performance is comparable to that of prompted or finetuned medium-sized LLM monitors, while offering computational savings of six orders-of-magnitude. Our experiments also highlight the potential of building resource-aware hierarchical monitoring systems, where probes serve as an efficient initial filter and flag cases for more expensive downstream analysis. We release our novel synthetic dataset and codebase to encourage further study.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of activation probes for detecting "high-stakes" interactions in Large Language Models (LLMs). High-stakes interactions are defined as situations where the LLM's outputs could have significant real-world consequences. The authors train several probe architectures on a synthetic dataset of high/low-stakes interactions and demonstrate their ability to generalize to diverse, real-world datasets. They compare the performance of these probes against prompted and fine-tuned LLM baselines and find that probes achieve comparable performance with significantly lower computational cost.  The paper also investigates a hierarchical monitoring system, using probes as an initial filter to identify potentially high-stakes cases for further analysis by more computationally expensive LLM classifiers. The authors release their synthetic dataset and code.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies in:

*   **Target Application:** Focusing on "high-stakes" interactions as a specific, practical, and largely unexplored area of LLM safety monitoring is a valuable contribution. While prior work has examined probes for deception, toxicity, etc., directly targeting high-stakes contexts is a distinct and relevant goal.
*   **Cost-Effectiveness Analysis:** The explicit comparison of probes to LLM-based monitors, especially in terms of computational cost, and advocating a hierarchical system is a valuable practical contribution.
*   **Synthetic Dataset:**  The creation of a dedicated synthetic dataset for training high-stakes detection models is a practical contribution.  The paper details the specific prompting strategies used to generate diverse and realistic high- and low-stakes interactions, addressing a common challenge in AI safety research.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance is determined by how significantly it influences the field and by its applicability in real-world LLM monitoring.

*   **Practical Impact:** The finding that activation probes can achieve performance comparable to larger LLMs at a fraction of the cost has significant implications for real-world deployment. It makes monitoring more feasible, especially in resource-constrained environments.  The hierarchical monitoring approach has significant practical applications, especially when combined with human-in-the-loop systems.
*   **Generalization and Robustness:** The paper demonstrates that probes trained on synthetic data can generalize to diverse, out-of-distribution, real-world datasets, which is a crucial requirement for any monitoring system. However, it also acknowledges the limitations, such as the sensitivity to humor, adversarial prompts, and the need for deployment-specific fine-tuning. The exploration into incorporating human review is positive.
*   **Limitations:**

    *   The paper heavily relies on GPT-4 to generate both training and evaluation labels. Although the authors include human validation, there's still a risk that the probes are simply learning to replicate GPT-4's interpretation of "high-stakes," rather than a more general concept. More exploration of bias within this process would be beneficial. Also, probes may be vulnerable to targeted adversarial attacks on the model.
    *   The evaluation scope could be expanded. While several real-world datasets are used, the assessment of the hierarchical monitoring system's effectiveness is somewhat limited.
*   **Limited performance on some datasets:** As noted in the paper the model did not perform well on all available datasets and the model could be sensitive to certain types of inputs.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper offers a valuable practical solution to a crucial problem in LLM safety. The computational efficiency of activation probes combined with a hierarchical monitoring approach holds substantial promise. However, the reliance on GPT-4 for labeling and the need for ongoing adaptation and refinement raise concerns.

Score: 7

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and valuable application of activation probes to the important problem of detecting high-stakes scenarios in LLMs. Its clear demonstration of cost-effectiveness and practical potential, with supporting data makes the paper well above average, but the use of a potentially biased GPT-4 labeling technique and the model's variable performance prevent the paper from receiving a higher rating. Future research should address the identified limitations to further strengthen its significance and impact.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Prompts to Summaries: Zero-Shot Language-Guided Video Summarization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10807v1)
- **Authors**: Mario Barbara, Alaa Maalouf
